#11009. 6 Best Coding Apps for Beginners to Learn Programming

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Learning to code can feel overwhelming at first. There are different programming languages, development tools, and concepts to understand, and choosing where to begin is not always easy. Coding apps can make the process more approachable by breaking lessons into smaller steps and giving beginners a place to practice what they learn.

Whether you want to build websites, develop software, or explore programming as a new skill, the right app can help you get started. Some platforms focus on interactive lessons, while others offer coding exercises, guided projects, or access to a full development environment.

Here are six coding apps worth considering if you're learning programming for the first time.

Mimo is a beginner-friendly coding app designed to help users learn programming through short lessons and interactive exercises. Rather than starting with lengthy explanations, it introduces concepts in manageable steps, making it easier to fit learning into a busy schedule.

The app covers several topics, including Python, JavaScript, HTML, CSS, and SQL. This makes it useful for beginners who want to explore general programming or focus on web development. As learners progress, they can work through structured learning paths and practice applying concepts through coding exercises and projects.

Mimo is particularly suitable for people who prefer a guided learning experience instead of figuring out what to study next on their own. Its mobile-first approach also makes it convenient to practice during breaks or while traveling.

A free starting option is available, although access to some courses and features requires a paid plan.

Best for: Beginners who want structured lessons and practical coding exercises.

SoloLearn is a coding education platform that helps beginners explore programming languages through short lessons, quizzes, and hands-on practice. It is a useful starting point for people who want to understand programming fundamentals before committing to a specific development path.

The platform offers courses in languages and technologies such as Python, JavaScript, Java, C++, SQL, HTML, and CSS. Its bite-sized lessons make complicated topics easier to approach, while interactive exercises let learners test their understanding as they progress.

Another useful feature is its learning community, where users can explore other people's code, participate in discussions, and learn from different approaches to programming problems.

SoloLearn works well for people who are still deciding which language to learn. However, completing lessons alone is not enough to develop strong programming skills. Beginners should also practice writing their own programs and building small projects.

The platform offers free learning options alongside paid features.

Best for: Beginners who want to explore multiple programming languages and learn through short, interactive lessons.

Codecademy Go is the mobile companion to Codecademy's online learning platform. It is designed to help learners reinforce programming concepts and review material while away from their computers.

Codecademy's broader learning platform offers courses covering web development, Python, JavaScript, SQL, and other technical subjects. The mobile app can be useful for reviewing concepts, practicing through supported activities, and maintaining a consistent learning routine between longer study sessions.

For example, someone taking a JavaScript course on a computer can use the mobile experience to revisit concepts and strengthen their understanding before returning to practical exercises.

It is important to distinguish between learning on a mobile device and developing software on one. Codecademy Go is primarily a companion for learning and review rather than a complete replacement for a desktop coding environment.

Some learning content is available without a subscription, while access to additional courses and features depends on the current plan.

Best for: Learners who want to review programming concepts alongside a structured online course.

Programming Hub is an educational app for people who want to explore programming and related technical subjects in one place. It brings together lessons, examples, and exercises designed to introduce users to different areas of software development.

Its learning material covers languages such as Python, Java, C++, and JavaScript, along with web development and other computing topics. This variety can be helpful for beginners who want to compare different languages before choosing one to study in depth.

The app's lesson-based approach makes it possible to explore a topic without immediately installing a development environment or configuring complicated software. Code examples and practice activities can also help learners connect theoretical concepts with practical applications.

However, beginners should avoid jumping between too many languages at once. Starting with one language, understanding its fundamentals, and applying those skills to a small project will generally provide a stronger foundation.

Programming Hub offers free and paid options, with access depending on the selected plan.

Best for: Beginners who want an introduction to several programming languages and computing topics.

Enki takes a slightly different approach to learning programming by emphasizing regular practice and the development of technical skills over time. Its lessons and exercises are designed to help users strengthen their understanding through consistent, manageable learning sessions.

Depending on the learning content available, users can explore topics such as Python, SQL, JavaScript, data analysis, and other areas of technology. This makes the app relevant to both aspiring developers and people who want to improve their technical knowledge for work.

One advantage of short, recurring practice sessions is that they encourage learners to revisit concepts instead of studying them once and moving on. Regular revision can help reinforce programming syntax, terminology, and problem-solving techniques.

Enki may be especially useful for people who already have some exposure to coding and want to maintain a learning habit. Complete beginners should check whether the available lessons match their chosen language and current skill level before starting.

The app offers different access options, so check its current listing for details about free and paid features.

Best for: Learners who prefer regular practice and gradual improvement in technical skills.

Replit is a browser-based development platform that allows users to write, run, and test code without setting up a traditional development environment on their computer. It is particularly useful for beginners who want to move beyond lessons and start creating their own programs.

Users can work with languages and technologies such as Python, JavaScript, HTML, and CSS, depending on the project and environment. Instead of only answering quiz questions, learners can experiment with code, identify errors, and see how their changes affect a working application.

For example, a beginner learning Python could create a simple calculator, while someone studying web development could build a basic personal website. Starting with small projects helps learners understand how individual programming concepts work together.

Replit also provides AI-assisted development features, which can help users explore ideas and understand code. However, beginners should review generated code and learn why it works rather than relying on automated suggestions without understanding them.

Replit offers a free starting option, with additional capabilities available through paid plans. Features and usage limits may vary.

Best for: Beginners who want to practice writing code and experiment with real projects in a development environment.

The best coding app depends on your learning goals, preferred learning style, and current experience. Before choosing one, consider what you want to accomplish.

Choose a language first. If you want to learn general programming, Python is a reasonable starting point. If your goal is to build websites, HTML, CSS, and JavaScript are more directly relevant.

Consider how you learn. Apps such as Mimo and SoloLearn emphasize short lessons and interactive practice. Codecademy Go is useful for reviewing course material, while Replit provides an environment for experimenting with code.

Look for practical exercises. Understanding a lesson is only the beginning. Choose resources that encourage you to write code, solve problems, and apply concepts in small projects.

Check the cost. Several coding apps provide free introductory content, but some courses and advanced features may require subscriptions. Review the current pricing before committing to a paid plan.

Move beyond the app. As your skills improve, practice using a computer, read technical documentation, and create projects of your own. These activities help you develop the problem-solving skills required for real programming work.

Coding apps provide an accessible way to begin learning programming without needing extensive technical knowledge or complicated software installations. They can help you understand basic concepts, practice regularly, and discover which areas of development interest you most.

Mimo and SoloLearn are worth exploring if you prefer guided lessons, while Codecademy Go can complement a structured course. Programming Hub and Enki offer alternative approaches to learning and practice, and Replit provides a way to experiment with working code.

You do not need to master every programming language or use several apps simultaneously. Start with one resource, focus on a single learning goal, and gradually apply what you learn to practical projects. Consistent practice and curiosity will take you further than simply completing lessons.

#11002. AI, DevOps and the Cost of Hosting: From Idea to Production for $0

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI coding agent rebuilt my photo-collage app as a distributed system and deployed it to a free server. The hard part turned out to be hardware: one free ARM CPU, 538 collages created during load testing, zero failed jobs, and $0 a month.

Like most developers, I have never been short of product ideas. Hosting them has always been the problem. I have tried shared hosting with SmartWeb, Azure free credits, Google Compute Engine’s free VM instances, SmartWeb’s virtual private servers, and Heroku dynos. None of them left me satisfied. The worst experience was a $200 charge for an AI address API on Google Cloud—for an application that never earned me a single kobo.

I am not a cheapskate, but I learned quickly that one of the easiest ways to kill a potentially good idea is to get the finances wrong. An application has to stay online until it reaches a critical mass of users, and hosting an untested, unverified product on expensive hardware can be wasteful.

Agentic AI coding has changed how quickly I can move from idea to production. Over about five weeks of intermittent coding—amounting to roughly three days of full work with Claude—my photo-stitching app went from an Angular 8 project that would not even build to a distributed system running in production.

Along the way, Claude upgraded the front end from Angular 8 to Angular 22 and fixed the build on Node 24. It set up RabbitMQ with Docker Compose so the message broker configures itself on startup, and added real-time notifications over SignalR with a progress spinner. It fixed a crash loop caused by an unreadable iPhone photo. It redesigned the system so any server can process any job, with switchable database and file storage, direct browser-to-storage uploads, and photos resized in the browser before upload. It added reliability work that a team might debate for a sprint: a dead-letter queue, confirmed publishing, idempotent workers, recovery of stuck jobs, and automatic clean-up. It fixed the stitching so photos keep their proportions and borders are even. It wrote 41 automated tests and scrubbed leaked passwords and personal photos from the Git history.

Coding, QA, DevOps, and production support used to be separate phases, often handled by separate people. Here they happened in one conversation. When code becomes this cheap to produce, the bottleneck moves. For me, it moved to hardware.

To check what the AI builds, you have to run it locally. A distributed app means running the whole system locally: a web app, a background worker, a message broker, a database, and storage emulators. Because Claude adds features quickly, and each one can add another container, it is easy to exhaust a laptop’s RAM and CPU.

My setup made this lesson concrete. I work on a MacBook Air, with Visual Studio in a Windows virtual machine. Docker cannot run inside that VM on Apple Silicon, so Claude ran Docker on the Mac and drove it from Windows over SSH. Later, my mobile connection started dropping large packets and broke uploads from the VM, so Claude moved the load testing onto the server itself. The software problems took minutes. The hardware and network problems took longer.

Then comes hosting. Heroku looked like the easy route, but it has had no free dynos since 2022. The cheapest option is an Eco dyno at about $5 a month, plus about $5 for Postgres. That is affordable, but running it for an idea that has no users yet did not seem prudent.

Claude suggested Oracle Cloud’s Always Free tier: an ARM server with up to 4 CPUs and 24 GB of RAM, free with no end date. I created a small instance with 1 CPU and about 6 GB. Claude wrote a production Docker Compose file and a one-command setup script: Caddy as the gateway, the web app, the stitching worker, RabbitMQ, and PostgreSQL, with data that survives restarts. My repository is private, so rather than put a GitHub token on a public server, it copied the code over SSH and deployed it. The last blocker was mine to fix: opening ports 80 and 443 in Oracle’s network firewall. Then the site was live.

I did not want to guess, so I asked Claude to load-test the live server. Simulated users ran the full flow: upload two photos, wait for the real-time notification, and download the stitched collage.

The server handled 64 people creating collages at the same moment without a single failed job: 538 collages, zero errors. Its ceiling was about 62 collages a minute—one per second. Beyond that, users queue. With 4 people creating at once, 95% of collages arrived within about five seconds; at 64, each person waited about a minute. If each active user makes a collage every couple of minutes, one CPU comfortably serves around 85 people at once, and a few hundred if they use it less often.

The test also showed what runs out first, and it is not the CPU. Each collage stores about 5 MB, mostly the PNG output, so at full speed the disk would fill in under two hours. Switching to JPEG and shortening retention fixes that, and when traffic grows, three more free CPUs are waiting. That is the value of measuring before paying: I know what to change, and when.

Oracle isn’t being charitable. Servers need applications to run—ideally ones that go viral—and the free tier is a play for loyalty. Oracle wants successful projects to stay and grow into its paid services instead of moving off the platform once they reach critical mass. It can also reclaim free instances that sit idle.

I think the bet is smart, and it points to a bigger trend. AI-generated code still needs energy to run and hardware to host it, even when far fewer people work on each project. As more people turn ideas into working software, demand for compute will keep growing, and I expect hosting and data centres to become a very profitable business.

For now, the combination works: Claude orchestrates the coding, QA, and DevOps, and Oracle’s free server keeps an idea online long enough to find out whether it deserves to grow.

#10996. Building a Human Review Pipeline for AI Search: A Practical Developer’s Guide

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI search feature can return valid JSON, render correctly, and still give an unhelpful answer.

Imagine a documentation assistant answering a question about an API's current authentication requirements. It retrieves an outdated page, summarizes it fluently, and displays a citation. Every technical component appears to work, but the user receives instructions that no longer apply.

Automated checks are valuable, yet they cannot replace a clearly defined judgment about whether an answer solves the user's problem.

This guide builds a human-review workflow for a hypothetical API documentation assistant. You will define a review rubric, validate submissions with Python, measure reviewer disagreement, and turn accepted judgments into regression cases. The Python examples use Pydantic 2 and scikit-learn; they do not require a model-provider API key.

Start by identifying the failure you want to detect.

A documentation assistant usually has several stages: receive a question, retrieve relevant documents, generate an answer, and present that answer with supporting material.

Do not collapse these into a single “good answer” rating. A low score without a reason tells the team little about what to fix.

SitePoint’s introduction to retrieval-augmented generation provides background on how retrieval and generation fit together.

Preserve the exact question, answer revision, retrieved passages, document versions, and retrieval timestamp for each run. Store a snapshot manifest with stable passage IDs, source URLs, and content hashes. Reviewers should see the material supplied to that run, not today’s edited page. Keep the model and prompt configuration with the run so a later review can be traced to the behavior it evaluated.

A useful rubric defines observable criteria and separates evidence support from task completion and source suitability. Give each review the same user task and expected API version. Otherwise, reviewers may judge different questions while appearing to disagree about one answer.

For an answer about API authentication, use separate labels such as:

Supported: every material factual claim is backed by the supplied evidence.

Partially supported: some material claims lack support, while others are grounded.

Unsupported: the answer's central instruction lacks support or contradicts the evidence.

Cannot assess: the necessary evidence is missing, unreadable, or inaccessible.

Define task completion independently as complete, incomplete, or cannot assess. For source suitability, use suitable, outdated, or cannot assess. An answer can faithfully summarize an obsolete page while failing the request for current instructions.

Consider a fictional API migration: version 2 accepts X-API-Key, while version 3 requires Authorization: Bearer . If the supplied evidence contains only the version 2 page, an answer repeating X-API-Key can be supported by its evidence but still use an outdated source. If the evidence explicitly requires Bearer and the answer invents X-API-Key, label the answer unsupported. These are different engineering failures.

Also establish what reviewers should do when the source is outdated. They may correctly conclude that an answer reflects its evidence while separately flagging that evidence as unsuitable for the user's current task.

Start with a calibration batch covering clear successes, unsupported claims, missing evidence, and version mismatches. Have two reviewers label the same items independently, then compare their reasons. Revise ambiguous definitions before increasing volume; the batch is for improving the rubric, not estimating production quality.

Keep a rubric version on every review. Labels produced under different definitions should not be mixed without checking comparability.

A spreadsheet can help during exploration, but an engineering pipeline needs a stable record format.

Pydantic can validate a review record before it enters the dataset. Install Pydantic 2 and scikit-learn in your project environment, then use this model:

from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict, Field, ValidationError

model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid", strict=True)

answer_revision: str = Field(min_length=1)

completion: Literal["complete", "incomplete", "cannot_assess"]

source_fit: Literal["suitable", "outdated", "cannot_assess"]

rationale: str = Field(min_length=20, max_length=2000)

Strict mode limits type coercion, and extra="forbid" rejects unexpected fields. The model checks shape and allowed values; it cannot establish that a judgment is correct. A minimum rationale length does not guarantee a useful explanation. The snapshot ID references a manifest, which can also record that retrieval produced no usable passages.

On the server, resolve the item, answer revision, snapshot, and passage IDs against the stored run. Reject IDs that do not belong together. Obtain the reviewer identity from the authenticated session rather than trusting a submitted reviewer_id. Store a server-generated submission time and preserve review revisions instead of silently overwriting earlier judgments.

Run this after the model definition. It accepts one fictional review and rejects both an unknown label and an extra field:

"rationale": "The answer invents X-API-Key; p1 requires Bearer.",

raise AssertionError("Invalid review was accepted")

The expected error types are literal_error and extra_forbidden. Add separate integration tests for mismatched evidence IDs, unauthorized submissions, and retries. Use a database uniqueness rule or an idempotency key so a repeated request does not create a second independent review.

More reviews do not automatically produce better evidence. If a task is ambiguous, scaling it can simply reproduce the ambiguity.

The workflow needs clear eligibility requirements, examples, escalation routes, and acceptance criteria. Reviewers assessing API behavior may need domain knowledge that general writing fluency does not provide.

If you work with an external AI-data contributor service such as Acquirox, define the task contract before assigning work: required expertise, permitted source material, output fields, and escalation rules. Keep ownership of the rubric, evaluation set, and acceptance decisions within the product team. Contributor capacity alone does not establish expertise in the API being reviewed.

Pilot the actual task in the review interface. Track drafts, submitted reviews, items needing adjudication, and final decisions as distinct workflow states. Reviewer judgments should remain immutable once submitted; corrections should create a new revision with a reason. Keep adjudication separate from the original labels.

For data involving users or contributors, document permitted uses, access boundaries, and retention. Remove credentials and unnecessary personal information from review material. An internal support transcript should not become a broadly accessible annotation task by accident.

Distinguish annotation from usability research. A skilled reviewer can judge whether an answer is supported, but that does not prove a first-time user can understand the interface.

SitePoint’s guide to choosing usability tests and participants offers related guidance on matching participants and methods to the question being investigated.

The review interface shapes the data it collects.

Label Studio is an option for configurable labeling interfaces and data import/export. If you use it, map exported annotations into the Review schema through an adapter; do not assume its export structure matches your application contract. A custom interface may be appropriate when reviewers need API-version navigation or passage-level citations.

The original question and relevant user context.

The evidence snapshot supplied to the model.

Separate controls for support and task completion.

A rationale field and an explicit “cannot assess” option.

Display stable passage IDs beside the evidence and let reviewers attach those IDs to their rationale. Show the source version and retrieval time near each passage. Render retrieved content as untrusted text or sanitized markup, and never treat instructions embedded in a source document as reviewer instructions. Avoid loading executable third-party content inside the review panel.

Avoid showing a preferred model name or a previous reviewer's verdict before an independent judgment. For pairwise comparisons, randomize answer order and include appropriate tie or neither-acceptable outcomes.

Design for keyboard use, visible focus, readable text, and explicit selection states. Keep evidence and the answer accessible without requiring excessive horizontal scrolling.

Start all judgment controls unselected and validate them on submission. Use labeled radio groups, an accessible error summary, and visible saving/submission status. A failed save should preserve the draft. Disable duplicate submissions in the UI, but enforce duplicate handling on the server as well.

Practical check: observe a reviewer completing several borderline examples. Record where they pause, misinterpret a label, or cannot find the necessary source material.

When two reviewers disagree, investigate before reducing their decisions to a majority vote.

Different assumptions about the user's expertise.

A genuine error by one or both reviewers.

Preserve independent labels and store the adjudicated result separately. This allows the team to study ambiguity instead of erasing it.

For two reviewers applying categorical labels to the same items, scikit-learn provides Cohen's kappa:

from sklearn.metrics import cohen_kappa_score

("q1", "a1", "s1", "auth-v1"): "supported",

("q2", "a1", "s2", "auth-v1"): "unsupported",

("q3", "a1", "s3", "auth-v1"): "supported",

("q4", "a1", "s4", "auth-v1"): "partially_supported"

("q3", "a1", "s3", "auth-v1"): "partially_supported"

raise ValueError("Review sets must contain identical items")

raw_agreement = sum(x == y for x, y in zip(left, right)) / len(keys)

print(f"Raw agreement: {raw_agreement:.2f}")

print(f"Kappa: {cohen_kappa_score(left, right):.2f}")

This toy example prints raw agreement of 0.75 and kappa of about 0.64. Four items are not enough to assess a review program. In production, check for duplicate reviews before building dictionaries, use one rubric version per comparison, report unmatched items, and align all records by item, answer revision, and evidence snapshot.

Kappa measures agreement beyond chance under its assumptions, not correctness. Inspect raw agreement, label frequencies, and the disagreement table alongside it. A single-category batch can make kappa undefined; report that limitation rather than substituting a reassuring score. Treat cannot_assess explicitly and report its frequency instead of silently excluding difficult cases.

If both reviewers apply the same flawed rubric consistently, agreement can be high while the evaluation remains unhelpful.

Once difficult cases are adjudicated, turn them into regression cases with explicit acceptance criteria. For the fictional authentication task: use version 3 evidence, cite the passage requiring Bearer, and do not invent a header. If version 3 evidence is missing, an acceptable response should explain the gap rather than provide confident instructions.

Expected behavior or acceptance criteria.

DVC can track larger datasets alongside Git. In an existing Git repository with DVC initialized and data/reviewed-auth.jsonl present, a minimal versioning step is:

git add data/reviewed-auth.jsonl.dvc data/.gitignore

git commit -m "Version authentication review cases"

This commits metadata, not the dataset bytes. Configure an access-controlled DVC remote and push the data before expecting another machine or CI runner to retrieve it. Keep credentials and sensitive review material out of public repositories.

Keep development examples separate from held-out evaluation cases. If developers repeatedly tune prompts against the same questions, those questions become less useful as evidence of generalization.

Split related examples together where appropriate. Near-duplicate questions from one support conversation should not be scattered across development and evaluation sets.

Use two evaluation passes. First, hold the evidence snapshot fixed to compare answer-generation changes. Then test retrieval against the intended document versions to detect stale or irrelevant sources. Record per-item outcomes and report failures by category; an overall improvement can hide a regression in version-specific or multilingual questions.

SitePoint’s article on testing nondeterministic AI behavior provides further context for repeatable evaluation workflows.

A correct answer can still fail in the interface.

Users may overlook a caveat, misunderstand whether an operation has been performed, or miss that the evidence applies to an older API version.

Pair answer review with practical usability tasks:

Find the authentication requirements for version 3 and identify the source that supports them.

Observe whether users complete the task and whether their confidence matches the available evidence.

SitePoint’s overview of user research methods helps distinguish task observation from preference surveys.

Turn interface failures into browser tests: a citation opens the expected document version, its passage ID matches the displayed evidence, missing evidence offers a recovery action, and a failed review submission preserves the draft. Verify that reviewers cannot access unassigned restricted items by changing an ID in the URL. These checks complement human judgments; they do not replace them.

Do not use a fluent response as a substitute for a clear interaction. The interface should distinguish retrieved information, generated interpretation, and actions the application has actually completed.

Start with one authentication task, a versioned rubric, and a small calibration batch. Use Pydantic for ingestion checks, Label Studio or a custom interface for review, scikit-learn for agreement analysis, and Git/DVC for dataset provenance. Add each tool only when it solves a concrete workflow need.

At release time, run deterministic checks for schema, evidence references, permissions, and interface behavior. Compare candidate answers against adjudicated acceptance criteria and send new ambiguous failures back for review. Record the dataset revision and configurations with the results so another developer can reproduce the comparison.

The purpose of human review is to produce evidence the team can act on. A smaller collection of well-defined, traceable judgments is often more useful than a large pile of ratings whose meaning nobody can explain.

#10989. How to Add AI Content Detection to Your Node.js App

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sooner or later someone on your team asks it. Maybe it’s a product manager, maybe the head of content, maybe a teacher who’s just marked forty near-identical essays: can we tell when people are pasting in ChatGPT output?

The short answer is no, not with certainty. Anyone who promises you that is selling something.

But you can get a useful signal, and you can show it to the people who actually make the call: editors, teachers, moderators. In this tutorial you’ll build a small Express app that takes some text, sends it to an AI detector API, waits for the result, and highlights the sentences that look machine-written.

I’m using the Quetext AI Detector API here. It scores each sentence, not just the whole document, and you can try it without a subscription. Most detection APIs follow the same submit, poll, fetch pattern, though. If you switch providers later, you’ll rewrite one file.

Put the key in an environment variable. Don’t hard-code it, and don’t let it anywhere near the browser.

You’ll make three calls. Analysis takes a few seconds, so the API hands you a ticket and makes you come back for the result. Keep the Quetext API docs open in another tab while you follow along; they list every endpoint, field and error code.

Every request sends your key in an X-API-Key header. The minimum input is 50 words, which sounds annoying until you realise no detector can say anything useful about a two-line comment.

And remember this number: 10 requests every 5 seconds, per account. Progress checks count. It’ll come back to bite you in the production section if you ignore it.

Create quetext.js. I like keeping everything provider-specific in one file, so the rest of the app has no idea who’s doing the detecting.

Three decisions in there that are worth a sentence each:

The errors keep their HTTP status code. A 402 means you’ve run out of credit and a 429 means you’re polling too fast, and you’ll want to handle those differently from “something broke.”

It polls every 2.5 seconds because Quetext’s docs suggest 2 to 3. Polling faster won’t speed the report up. You’ll just burn through the rate limit.

And there’s a hard timeout. Leave it out and one stuck report can hang a request forever. That’s a miserable bug to track down at 11pm.

Next, server.js. It checks the input, runs the three calls, and gives the frontend a simple response that doesn’t mention Quetext anywhere.

Look at the mapping at the end. generated_prob becomes probability, and ai_score comes back as a string like "82.50", so it gets parsed into a number. It’s a small detail. But it means your frontend never learns which provider’s field names it’s dealing with, and that’s what makes swapping providers a one-file change.

Show someone “64% AI” and they’ll jump to a conclusion. Show them a paragraph with three sentences shaded pink and they’ll actually read it.

So the page shows every sentence, coloured by how likely it is to be AI-generated. Create public/index.html:

Run node server.js and open http://localhost:3000. Try two samples: something you wrote yourself, then a raw chatbot answer on the same topic. Seeing which sentences light up teaches you more about how these detectors work than any diagram I could draw.

A quick security point. Those sentences came from your users, then went through an outside service. Treat them as untrusted. That’s why the code uses textContent and never innerHTML, as the OWASP XSS prevention cheat sheet recommends.

The demo keeps an HTTP request open while it polls. Fine on your laptop. Not fine with real traffic.

Submit the text, save the report id with a pending status, and respond straight away. A worker (BullMQ, a cron job, a serverless queue, whatever you already use) does the polling and updates the record once it’s done. The UI says “Checking…” until the result shows up.

Here’s how it goes wrong. It’s Monday morning, 20 editors open their review queues and click “Check” at roughly the same time. Each request polls on its own, and within a few seconds you’ve hit 429s across the board, because the 10-per-5-seconds limit covers your whole account, not each user.

Use one worker that loops through every pending report, with a short pause between calls. Problem solved.

You pay per word, so don’t check every blog comment. Article submissions, assignments and paid freelance work usually justify the cost. Cache results against a hash of the text too. Someone who submits the same draft three times shouldn’t cost you three times.

AI detection doesn’t catch copying. Someone can lift a 2019 blog post word for word and it’ll score 0% AI, because a human wrote it. Just not this human. If originality matters to your product, the same API has plagiarism endpoints that work exactly the same way. The API documentation covers both.

Developers skip this part. It’s also the part that ends up in angry support tickets.

In 2023, Liang and colleagues at Stanford ran essays written by non-native English speakers (all human, written for the TOEFL) through seven AI detectors. On average, the detectors labelled more than 61% of them as AI-generated. Simple vocabulary and textbook grammar read as “predictable,” and predictability is exactly what detectors look for.

Picture an international student getting an automatic zero because a script said so. You don’t want to be the person who built that.

Do that and the detector is a fast first pass that points people at what’s worth a closer look. Your humans still make the call.

You send text, the service checks how predictable each sentence would be to a language model, and you get back a probability that a machine wrote it. Most of these APIs are asynchronous. You submit, get a report ID, poll until it’s ready, then fetch the result. The better ones score individual sentences as well as the whole text, which makes building a sensible review screen much easier.

More than you’d think. Quetext’s API won’t accept fewer than 50 words, and in practice scores settle down once you’re past about 300. A product title or a one-line comment just doesn’t give a detector enough to go on. For short content, either group related pieces together or don’t bother checking it.

No. Detectors make mistakes, and research shows they flag non-native English writers far more often than native speakers. Use the score to decide what a human looks at first, not to block anything. Give reviewers the highlighted sentences, give users a way to explain themselves, and log what you decided. Your users get treated fairly and your support team avoids a pile of angry tickets.

Poll at the speed the provider recommends (every 2 to 3 seconds for Quetext), and remember that those polls count against your limit too. Once you’re in production, move checks into a background queue and let one worker poll every pending report, rather than having each user request poll by itself. When you do get a 429, wait a moment and retry.

No. If the browser calls the API directly, your key ends up in the frontend code where anyone can copy it and spend your credit. Keep the key in an environment variable on your server and send requests through your own endpoint, like the one in this tutorial. That also gives you somewhere to check input length, apply per-user limits and cache results before you pay for anything.

#10973. How to Optimize AI-Generated Images for Faster Websites

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated images are becoming a common part of modern web development. Developers use them for website banners, product illustrations, landing pages, blog posts, and digital interfaces. However, generating an image is only one part of the process. Before publishing it, developers need to ensure that the image loads quickly, displays correctly across devices, and maintains its visual quality.

Large image files can increase page size, consume unnecessary bandwidth, and delay the appearance of important content. These problems can affect user experience and website performance, particularly on mobile devices and slower internet connections.

A reliable image optimization workflow helps address these challenges. By selecting appropriate dimensions, choosing efficient image formats, automating image processing, and implementing responsive delivery, developers can improve loading performance without sacrificing visual quality.

This guide explains how to optimize AI-generated images using practical techniques and development tools.

Images often account for a significant portion of a webpage's total download size. Uploading large, unoptimized images can make pages slower and increase the amount of data visitors need to download.

Several common problems contribute to poor image performance:

Incorrect dimensions waste bandwidth on smaller screens.

Missing image dimensions can cause unexpected layout shifts.

Poor responsive configuration can make browsers download unnecessarily large images.

Incorrect loading priorities can delay important visual content.

These issues can affect Core Web Vitals, including Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), which measures how quickly the largest visible content element appears.

For developers, image optimization should therefore be part of the website development workflow rather than a final task performed immediately before publication.

Before optimizing an image, determine where it will appear on the website. A full-width banner requires different dimensions from a product thumbnail or a small interface graphic.

For example, a website might use the following dimensions:

These dimensions are examples rather than fixed requirements. The appropriate size depends on the website layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

When preparing source visuals, developers can explore different AI image generation options and consider how the generated images will fit their intended layouts.

Once the image is ready, keep a high-quality master file. This allows developers to create smaller versions for different screen sizes without repeatedly compressing an already optimized image.

Avoid enlarging small images unnecessarily because upscaling can introduce blur and visible artifacts.

Choosing the right format can reduce image size while preserving acceptable visual quality.

Three commonly used formats are AVIF, WebP, and JPEG.

AVIF: Often provides efficient compression for photographs and detailed illustrations. It can significantly reduce file sizes, although encoding may take longer.

WebP: Supports lossy and lossless compression, transparency, and animation. It offers broad browser support and is suitable for many website images.

JPEG: Remains useful for photographs and situations where compatibility with older software or systems is important.

PNG is appropriate for certain lossless graphics, screenshots, and images that require transparency. However, it can produce unnecessarily large files when used for photographic content.

No format is automatically the best choice for every image. Developers should compare file sizes and inspect visual quality before selecting the final output.

A practical approach is to generate AVIF and WebP versions and provide a JPEG fallback where necessary.

Manually resizing and converting images becomes time-consuming when a website contains dozens or hundreds of visual assets.

Automation makes the process more consistent. Developers can use Sharp, a Node.js image-processing library, to resize images, convert formats, and generate multiple versions from a single source file.

Next, create a file named build-images.mjs:

The script creates multiple image sizes in both WebP and AVIF formats.

The quality settings are starting points, not universal recommendations. Developers should adjust them after checking the resulting file sizes and visual details.

This approach is particularly useful for content-heavy websites, product catalogs, and applications that publish images regularly.

Responsive image delivery allows browsers to select an appropriate image based on the visitor's screen and the space available in the layout.

HTML provides two important attributes for this purpose: srcset and sizes.

The srcset attribute lists the available image files and their widths. The sizes attribute describes how wide the image is expected to appear at different viewport sizes.

In this example, the image is expected to occupy approximately 720 pixels on wider screens and nearly the full viewport width on smaller screens.

The browser uses this information to choose an appropriate image candidate.

One common mistake is setting sizes="100vw" for an image that occupies only a small part of the page. This can encourage the browser to download a larger file than necessary.

Always ensure that the sizes attribute reflects the actual website layout.

The HTML picture element allows developers to provide multiple image formats and a fallback option.

The browser selects a supported format, while the img element provides the fallback image and alternative text.

Developers should verify that every referenced file exists and that its dimensions match the intended layout.

The picture element can also support different crops for desktop and mobile devices. This is useful when a wide banner needs a separate composition for smaller screens.

Images without defined dimensions can cause content to move while the browser loads the file. This behavior contributes to Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS).

Including width and height attributes helps browsers reserve the appropriate space before the image finishes loading.

For responsive layouts, developers can use CSS:

This allows images to scale within their containers while maintaining their proportions.

Images below the initial viewport can generally use native lazy loading:

However, the main hero image should not normally be lazy-loaded when it is responsible for LCP. Delaying an important above-the-fold image can make the page appear slower.

Where appropriate, developers can use fetchpriority="high" for the main image to help the browser prioritize its download.

The correct strategy depends on the image's position and importance within the page.

Image optimization involves more than file size and dimensions. Developers should also consider accessibility, file organization, and metadata.

Alternative text helps people using screen readers understand meaningful images. It should describe the image's purpose in the context of the page.

Decorative images that provide no useful information should generally use an empty alt attribute.

File names should also be descriptive. Names such as home-office-sunrise.webp are easier to understand and maintain than generic names such as image-final-v3.png.

Metadata requires additional consideration. Image-processing tools may remove metadata by default to reduce file size. This can include information that a project needs to preserve, depending on its requirements.

Before removing metadata, developers should review whether the website or client requires attribution, provenance information, or other embedded details.

After implementing an optimization workflow, test the actual website rather than relying only on exported file sizes.

Chrome DevTools provides useful information about image requests.

Open Developer Tools and select the Network panel.

Resize the viewport or use device emulation.

Check whether the browser downloads appropriately sized images.

Developers can also inspect the image selected by the browser through the console:

This returns the current image URL selected for that element.

For websites with multiple images, inspect the relevant elements individually.

Finally, run an audit using Lighthouse or PageSpeed Insights. These tools can identify issues involving oversized images, image formats, loading priorities, and other performance factors.

Image optimization is only one part of website performance. Server response times, caching, CSS, JavaScript, and third-party scripts can also influence loading speed.

Several mistakes can reduce the effectiveness of an image optimization workflow:

Uploading original images without checking their dimensions.

Assuming one format always produces the smallest file.

Omitting the sizes attribute from responsive images.

Lazy-loading important above-the-fold images.

Applying excessive compression and damaging visual details.

Removing metadata without checking project requirements.

Measuring file size without inspecting the final visual quality.

A consistent testing process helps identify these issues before they affect visitors.

No. AVIF often provides efficient compression, but the results depend on the image, encoding settings, and quality requirements. Comparing both formats is the best way to choose an appropriate option.

Four to six sizes may be sufficient for many websites. The ideal number depends on the layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

Yes. Reducing the size of the main content image, providing responsive versions, and avoiding unnecessary loading delays can improve LCP when that image is the largest contentful element.

No. Lazy loading is useful for images outside the initial viewport. Important images near the top of the page should generally load promptly.

The fundamental techniques are the same as those used for conventional images. However, AI-generated visuals may contain complex textures, gradients, and fine details that require careful inspection after compression.

AI-generated images can improve website design and make it easier to create custom visual content. However, efficient delivery requires more than exporting an image and uploading it to a website.

Developers should choose suitable dimensions, compare modern image formats, automate processing, and implement responsive image delivery. Including image dimensions helps prevent layout shifts, while appropriate loading priorities can improve the experience for visitors.

By testing images across different devices and reviewing their actual performance, development teams can maintain visual quality while reducing unnecessary downloads.

A repeatable optimization workflow makes it easier to publish new visuals without sacrificing website speed, accessibility, or reliability.

“It is with extreme sadness that we share with you the devastating news that Fiona did not make it after multiple attempts from our amazing vets and keeper team to help her through what ended up being a medical emergency,” the zoo wrote.

A post shared by Cincinnati Zoo (@cincinnatizoo)

The zoo confirmed that Fiona received necessary supportive care on November 19 in a final attempt to save her life, but the treatment was unsuccessful.

Fiona became an international sensation after being born prematurely at the Cincinnati Zoo in January 2017. Weighing just 29 pounds at birth, she required round-the-clock care from zoo staff before eventually growing into one of the facility’s most recognizable animals.

#10965. How to Test AI-Generated Content Before It Reaches Your Users

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most teams now ship something an AI helped write: help-center articles, product descriptions, release notes, even code comments. The draft appears in seconds, and that speed is the trap. Nobody tests it the way they would test a new feature, so mistakes slip through that a quick review would have caught.

This guide is about the other side of AI testing. Instead of using AI to test your software, you test what AI hands you. Here is a simple routine that fits into a normal publishing or release process.

AI writing sounds confident whether or not it is right. A model can invent a statistic (researchers call this hallucination and have documented it across many text-generation systems), quote a feature your product does not have, or describe a setting that was renamed two releases ago. The sentence reads smoothly, so the error hides in plain sight.

Traditional QA does not cover this. A unit test tells you the page loads. It says nothing about whether the claim on the page is true. That gap is where most AI-related incidents begin.

1. Define what "good" means before you generate anything. Write down the audience, the claims you are willing to make and the sources you trust. A short brief makes the output easier to judge, because you are comparing it with something concrete.

2. Check every factual claim. Highlight each number, name and statement of fact, then verify it against a primary source. Google's own guidance on generative AI content tells publishers to fact-check AI output, including titles, descriptions and alt text, before publishing. If you cannot confirm a claim in a minute or two, cut it or rewrite it as something you can support.

3. Test the instructions. For tutorials and how-to content, follow the steps exactly as written on a clean machine or account. Commands that fail and menus that no longer exist show up immediately this way.

4. Review the voice. Replace vague phrases with specifics from your own experience: a real customer question, a real error message, a real number from your analytics. This is also what separates useful content from filler.

5. Keep a human owner. Every piece should have a named person who approved it. The NIST AI Risk Management Framework makes the same point at organization level: people stay accountable for how AI is used and evaluated. If something goes wrong later, you know who to ask and where to fix it.

Some teams also run drafts through an AI detector. That can be useful as a prompt for a closer look, for example when freelancers or vendors deliver work and you want to know how much of it was machine-generated. A high score is a reason to read more carefully. It is not proof of anything, and it should never decide an outcome on its own, because detectors make mistakes in both directions. A Stanford study found that GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers, and short texts are easy to misjudge too. If you want a quick second opinion on a draft, a tool like Tester AI can give you a starting point for that conversation.

Review works best when it is not optional. A few small habits make it stick.

Treating the first draft as finished. It is a starting point. Plan time for review the same way you plan time for writing.

Reviewing for grammar only. Clean sentences are not evidence of correct content. Check meaning first and polish second.

Letting the tool grade its own work. Asking the same model to confirm its answer rarely catches real errors. Use a source or a person.

Skipping legal and brand review. Generated text can echo existing material or make promises your company cannot keep. Anything customer-facing deserves a second pair of eyes.

AI can save hours of drafting, but speed only helps if the result is trustworthy. Treat generated content like code from a new contributor: useful, often good, and always reviewed before it goes live. A short routine of defining quality, verifying facts, testing instructions and keeping a human owner will catch most problems before your users do.

Do I need to test AI-generated content if a human edits it? Yes. Editing for style does not confirm that the facts are correct. Verify claims separately from polishing the wording.

How long should a review take? For a typical article, expect roughly the time it takes to read it twice plus a few minutes per factual claim. Reviews are faster when the brief and sources are prepared in advance.

Can an AI detector prove that text was written by a machine? No. Detectors estimate likelihood, and they can be wrong. Use the result as a signal for human review, never as the final decision.

What should I check first? Start with numbers, names, dates and anything a customer could act on, such as prices, steps or legal statements. Those carry the highest risk if they are wrong.

#10961. GTA 6 glitch lets players access first-person mode

A GTA 6 player has discovered a glitch that lets fans experience Rockstar’s new open-world game in first-person, despite the feature being unavailable at launch.

Grand Theft Auto 6 finally launched on November 19, but unlike GTA 5, the game doesn’t include a dedicated first-person mode.

Rockstar previously explained that development was focused on third-person animations, although players can still use a first-person perspective in certain situations.

However, just hours after release, a player discovered a workaround that allows them to explore Vice City through the eyes of Jason and Lucia, complete with weapon animations.

A GTA 6 player discovered a method/glitch to play in first-person mode, which seems to be functional.– Settings → Controller → Touch Pad Button → change to Camera– Sit in the driver’s seat and pull out the phone by pressing ↑ on the d-pad– Put your phone away by pressing… pic.twitter.com/W06K0Bb4Ft

The discovery was shared to YouTube by ‘oneboyler,’ detailing the find.

On PS5, players need to change their touchpad settings from opening the map to switching cameras. They must then enter a vehicle, open their in-game phone, and rapidly press Circle and the touchpad to trigger the glitch.

If successful, the camera becomes stuck in first-person, allowing players to leave their vehicle and explore the world without returning to the traditional third-person view.

The footage shows the player walking around and using weapons, with animations already appearing to support the perspective.

“As you can see, the weapon animations for first person mode are already in the game,” the uploader explained.

“This is the only way to experience GTA VI in first person mode, since the game for whatever reason didn’t release with it enabled.”

There is one major drawback, however. Once the glitch is activated, players cannot simply switch back to third-person. Instead, they must reload their save.

The latest discovery suggests that some first-person functionality remains accessible in the finished game, even if Rockstar hasn’t officially enabled it.

It’s unclear whether the developers plan to introduce a complete first-person mode in a future update or patch the glitch to prevent players from accessing it.

For now, it’s another discovery from GTA 6’s launch day, which has already seen fans uncover Dexter Morgan’s apartment and his signature killing tools hidden in Vice City.

#10957. The Hidden Problems Behind Website-to-CRM Integrations (and How to Prevent Them)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Connecting a website form to a customer relationship management (CRM) system may seem straightforward. A developer collects information from a form, sends it to an API endpoint, and checks whether a new contact appears in the CRM.

The integration may work perfectly during the first test. However, problems can appear after the website goes live. Some leads may not reach the CRM, duplicate contacts may appear, or important customer information may be missing.

These issues are not always easy to notice. A visitor might see a confirmation message even when the CRM has failed to save their information.

This guide explains common problems in website-to-CRM integrations and practical ways developers can prevent them.

A website-to-CRM integration allows information collected through a website to move into a CRM system. This usually happens through an API or webhook.

For example, when someone completes a contact form, the website collects their name, email address, phone number, and other details. The integration then sends this information to the CRM, where it can be stored and used by the relevant team.

An API integration provides a structured way for two applications to exchange information. Developers who need additional background can also read OpenHerald's overview of API integration.

The process usually involves several steps:

The website validates the submitted information.

The application sends a request to the CRM.

The CRM checks the request and processes the data.

The website or backend handles the response.

Each step needs to work correctly. If one part fails, the information may not reach its destination.

One important rule is to avoid displaying a successful submission message before the application has safely accepted and stored the data.

Data issues are among the most frequent problems in website-to-CRM integrations. They may not appear during development because test submissions usually contain simple, predictable information.

Real visitors enter data in different formats and may submit the same form more than once.

A visitor might complete a contact form and later download a resource using the same email address. If the integration creates a new CRM record every time, the same person may appear several times.

To prevent this, developers should decide how existing contacts will be identified.

Email addresses are commonly used for matching. Before comparing them, the application should remove unnecessary spaces and convert the address to a consistent format.

These values should be treated consistently.

Where supported, an upsert operation can create a new contact or update an existing one instead of creating unnecessary duplicates.

Website forms and CRM systems do not always use the same field structure.

A website may collect a visitor's full name in one field, while the CRM expects separate first-name and last-name values. Other fields may have different names or requirements.

Developers should create a clear mapping between website fields and CRM fields before building the integration.

They should also check which fields are required. If the CRM requires information that the website does not collect, the request may fail.

Different systems may store information in different formats.

For example, one application might use United Kingdom, while another expects UK. Dates, phone numbers, and country values can also create problems.

A reliable integration should validate and standardise these values before sending them to the CRM.

A contact record is more useful when the sales or marketing team knows where the visitor came from.

However, integrations sometimes send only basic contact information and leave out details such as campaign source, landing page, and referral information.

Developers should identify which tracking fields are useful and include them in the integration where appropriate.

Even an integration that works correctly at launch can stop working later.

Authentication credentials may expire, permissions may change, or an API provider may limit the number of requests an application can make.

Three areas deserve particular attention.

Many APIs use access tokens or other credentials to verify requests. Some tokens expire and must be refreshed.

If an application does not handle token renewal correctly, requests may begin failing without an obvious problem on the website.

Developers should implement the authentication process recommended by the CRM provider and monitor failed requests.

An API provider may restrict the number of requests allowed within a particular period.

A marketing campaign can suddenly increase website traffic and generate more form submissions than usual. If the integration exceeds the provider's limit, some requests may be rejected.

The application should recognise rate-limit responses and retry requests according to the provider's instructions.

An integration should not receive more access than it needs.

For example, a form submission service may only need permission to create or update contacts. Giving it full administrative access increases unnecessary security risks.

Use the minimum permissions required for the integration to function.

External services can experience outages, network delays, and temporary errors. If the website sends a form submission directly to the CRM without storing it first, a failed request could mean a lost lead.

A safer approach is to separate receiving a submission from delivering it to the CRM.

Validate the form information on the server.

Store the submission safely in your own system.

Send it to the CRM through a background process.

Retry temporary failures after an appropriate delay.

Flag permanent failures for manual review.

This approach gives the application another opportunity to deliver information when the CRM becomes available again.

A request might reach the CRM successfully, but the response could be lost because of a network problem. The website may then retry the same request, creating a duplicate contact.

Idempotency helps prevent this situation. The application can use a stable identifier for each submission so that repeating the same operation does not create another record.

For example, a combination of the form ID and a normalised email address may help identify repeated submissions. The exact approach depends on the CRM's API capabilities.

This is an illustrative example. Functions such as crmUpsertContact() and scheduleRetry() need to be implemented according to the application and CRM being used.

The important point is that every submission should have a known status: successfully delivered, waiting for another attempt, or requiring manual attention.

A working integration should also protect the quality and security of the information it collects.

Public forms can receive automated submissions from bots. Without appropriate protection, these entries can fill the CRM with unwanted records.

Validation should happen on the server, even if the website already checks information in the browser.

Some forms collect permission to send marketing communications.

If a visitor selects a consent checkbox, that information should also be passed to the CRM. Otherwise, the CRM may contain the contact details without a reliable record of the visitor's choice.

Where relevant, store the consent value, the wording or version displayed, and the time the visitor gave consent.

An integration should only send the information needed for its intended purpose.

Collecting unnecessary personal details increases the amount of information the application must protect and maintain.

Developers should also understand where submitted information is stored, including databases, queues, application logs, and the CRM.

Testing an integration before launch is important, but monitoring it afterwards is just as necessary.

A successful test does not prove that every future submission will work.

The following checks can help identify problems early.

Compare the number of website form submissions with the number of contacts successfully created or updated in the CRM.

A growing difference between the two numbers may indicate that some requests are failing.

Application logs should record the reason a request failed and include a submission identifier where possible.

Avoid storing complete personal details in logs unless there is a specific, justified need.

Developers should configure notifications when the integration's failure rate exceeds an acceptable threshold.

This makes it easier to investigate problems before they affect a large number of submissions.

A scheduled test submission can help confirm that the complete process is still working.

For example, a test lead can be sent periodically and checked to confirm that it reaches the CRM correctly.

Someone should be responsible for monitoring the integration.

This person or team should review errors, check API changes, monitor credentials, and investigate changes to CRM fields.

Without a clear owner, small problems can remain unnoticed for a long time.

Documentation makes integrations easier to maintain and troubleshoot.

It does not need to be complicated. A short reference document covering the main rules can save considerable time when another developer needs to work on the system.

Before deployment, developers should also test unusual situations.

Testing these situations helps reveal weaknesses that ordinary form submissions may not expose.

What is the most common website-to-CRM integration problem?

Duplicate contacts and incorrect field mapping are common problems. They usually happen when the website and CRM handle data differently.

How can developers prevent duplicate contacts?

Normalise email addresses, use a consistent matching rule, and use an upsert operation or idempotency mechanism where the CRM supports it.

Should a website send form data directly to a CRM?

For more reliable handling, the application should first validate and safely store the submission. A background process can then deliver it to the CRM and retry temporary failures.

How can I tell if a CRM integration has stopped working?

Compare website submission counts with CRM records, monitor failed requests, and schedule regular test submissions.

Who should be responsible for maintaining the integration?

Assign a developer or technical team to monitor the connection, review errors, maintain credentials, and respond to API changes.

Developers can make these integrations more reliable by validating information on the server, storing submissions before delivery, using safe retry mechanisms, and monitoring the results after launch.

The goal is simple: every valid submission should reach the CRM, and every failed submission should be visible and recoverable rather than silently lost.

#10954. “Determined” woman completes 7 marathons in a year while pregnant

A woman has revealed that she completed seven marathons in 2026 despite being pregnant for a few of them, admitting that it was a bit “bizarre” at times.

For a lot of people, running a marathon is a goal that they work towards for months, if not years. Some want to set an impressive time, while others just want to be able to say they’ve completed the 26.2 miles.

In the case of Deirdre Keane, the 38-year-old is a seasoned long-distance runner, completing more than 60 marathons to this point. However, throughout 2026, she has been running marathons with an additional hurdle to deal with: she has been pregnant throughout.

“I knew how bizarre it was to be running a marathon at 34 weeks, but I also knew how fit I was and self-aware of my body at this point,” she told the Associated Press, admitting it is a “very unusual” challenge, but noted that she isn’t the first woman to do so.

As per the AP, the 38-year-old had signed up for 11 races in 2026, including the seven major marathons: Berlin, Boston, Chicago, London, New York, Sydney and Tokyo.

“I was like, this is going to be my last hurrah before settling down and becoming pregnant, becoming a mother,” Keane added. However, she didn’t discover she was pregnant until after running the Tokyo marathon, when she noticed her pace had started to slip.

“I don’t think she thought I would do them towards the end, especially being so pregnant at New York. But I am very stubborn and determined,” Keane added. “Plus, I got lucky; I was feeling good. So I was able to do it.”

#10951. Top 5 AI Animation Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A few years ago, animating a ten-second scene meant storyboards, rigging and a lot of patience. Today you can type a sentence, upload a drawing or point at a reference clip and get moving footage back in a minute or two. The results are uneven, but they are good enough that designers, marketers and indie developers now use them for storyboards, product teasers, game trailers and social content.

The catch is that these tools are not interchangeable. Some excel at cinematic camera moves, some at editing footage you already have, and some at a particular visual style. This guide compares five AI animation generators, what each one is best at and where it falls short. Plans, credits and model names change quickly, so check each vendor's pricing page before you commit.

Runway is a cloud workspace for generating and editing video, images and audio in one place. Its current video model is Gen-4.5, which Runway promotes for motion quality, prompt adherence and visual fidelity. The platform also offers agent and workflow tools, mobile apps for iPhone and Android, and an API for developers.

Best for: Creators who want to generate a shot and then keep refining it in the same environment, and teams that need an API.

Limitations: There is a lot to learn, and credit costs add up when you iterate. Runway offers a free trial, but check the pricing page for current limits.

Kling AI is an AI video and image generator with a creative studio for image generation, video generation and motion control. It also includes an agent for brainstorming and scripting, and a node-based canvas for working through scripts, storyboards, images and video with multiple rounds of editing. Kling lists advertising, film, music videos and e-commerce among its use cases, and offers an API platform and automation options for producing video at scale.

Best for: Marketers and studios that want to move from script to storyboard to footage in a single workspace.

Limitations: The feature set is broad, so newcomers may need time to find the right workflow. Free credits were not described on the pages I checked, so confirm what is available before relying on it.

Luma focuses on turning still images into video with camera motion. Its workspace can create transitions between frames, extend clips, transform footage into new styles, reframe aspect ratios and add voice, music, captions and lip sync. It runs Luma's own Ray models and also gives access to several third-party models, so you can compare results without switching platforms.

Best for: Designers and illustrators who already have key art, product shots or concept images and want to bring them to life.

Limitations: Paid plans start at around $30 a month at the time of writing, and generation credits can run out quickly if you test many variations.

Pika is a creative platform covering video, images and audio. Its Video Studio can produce anything from a short clip to a multi-shot video of around 30 seconds, and its toolkit includes Extend Video, Motion Transfer, Swap Anything and Color Grade. Character Studio lets you create reusable characters, and separate studios handle music, speech and sound effects. Pika also exposes several video models, including its own Pika 2.5 alongside third-party options.

Best for: Social content creators who want effects, sound and edits in one tool.

Limitations: With so many tools and models, results vary from one to the next. Check the current pricing page for credit details.

Pollo AI is a generator that leans into one look: anime. Its animation generator creates anime-style videos from a text prompt and lets you adjust the video content, output style and size. The page offers eleven Japanese animator-inspired style options, such as Hayao Miyazaki and Makoto Shinkai, and the platform also provides a separate tool that converts existing video into anime.

Best for: Creators who want a consistent anime look for shorts, fan projects, music visuals or social posts without learning a full editing suite.

Limitations: The generator page describes text prompts as its input and does not state clip length, resolution or credit details, so test a few generations and review the terms before planning a project around it. As with any style-focused tool, the look is distinctive, which is a strength for anime projects and a limit for anything else.

Start with the job rather than the brand. If you need to edit and refine footage, Runway is a natural fit. If you want a script-to-video pipeline, look at Kling. If you have strong still images, try Luma. If you want effects and sound for social posts, Pika covers a lot of ground. If anime is the goal, Pollo AI is built for it.

Run the same prompt through two or three tools before paying for anything. Differences in motion, faces and text rendering show up quickly, and they are often more important than the feature list.

AI animation tools have moved from novelty to practical production aid. None of them replaces a skilled animator, but each can speed up storyboards, concepts and short-form content when matched to the right job. Try a few with a real project, compare the output, and keep a human in charge of the final cut. If you want a quick way to experiment with anime styles, you can create anime clips from a prompt and see how the results compare with the other tools on this list.

Are AI animation generators free? Most offer a free trial or limited credits, with paid plans for more generations and higher quality. Terms change often, so read each pricing page.

Can I use AI-generated animation commercially? It depends on the provider's terms and your plan. Check the licensing section before publishing client work.

Do I need animation experience? No, but knowing basic shot composition helps you write better prompts and judge the results.

Which tool is best for beginners? Pick one with a simple prompt-first workflow, test the free tier and move to a more advanced tool as your needs grow.

#10949. University launches $5m smart system that checks who is actually listening in class

A university in China has rolled out a $5 million surveillance system that actually checks if people are paying attention in class.

Plenty of schools across the globe have embraced technology in their classrooms. Some of these have been as simple as letting kids use iPads and computers, while other schools are fully focused on teaching students about AI. Others are even using AI-powered robots to help them teach.

So, these days, it can be pretty difficult to slack off, not pay attention to what your teacher is saying, and then grab some notes from your friend.

Over in China, though, things have been taken to the next level when it comes to checking on who is actually paying attention to the lessons.

As per the South China Morning Post, Nanjing University, in Jiangsu province, has rolled out a smart classroom system that monitors students in different ways.

The system, which cost just under $5 million, tracks attendance and lateness, but is also able to check the “head-raising rate” of students as well. If you’re not looking up at the teacher and paying attention, the system will recognise that and count it against you.

According to SCMP, some students have rallied against the system on social media, forcing the university to scrap the tracking of head-raising rates. However, that hasn’t stopped other schools from going ahead with it.

SCMP noted that the China University of Mining and Technology in Jiangsu tracks the “classroom status” of students. This identified students who checked their phones during class, as well as flagging some as being asleep – even though they had been collecting notes.

High school forced to scrap AI security as it flags everything as weapons

South Korea offers $75,000 bounty to expose cheats on national college exam

Man admits to cheating on job exam after getting top score and sparking national investigation

Over in Pennsylvania, a high school was forced to scrap its test of an AI-powered security system.

The Wi-AI detection system from CurvePoint AI flagged binders, water bottles, and batteries as weapons, causing disruptions for students.
