#10895. Retrying a send is harder than sending

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every integration that moves something out of your system has the same weak point, and it is

not the happy path. It is the second attempt.

Your code posts a document to a provider. The connection drops before the response arrives. You now hold no job id, no confirmation and no idea whether anything left the building. The timeout told you about your network. It told you nothing about theirs.

So you retry, and you either send the thing twice or you never send it at all. Pick one, because

without help from the API you cannot have both.

The first instinct is a client-side guard. Hash the payload, keep a table of what you have already

That works until the duplicate attempt comes from a different process, a different container, or a

queue worker that started after a deploy. Your table is local state defending against a distributed

The second instinct is to ask the provider whether the job exists. That means listing recent jobs

and matching on something, usually the recipient and a timestamp window. It is a search, and

searches have false positives. Two legitimate sends to the same recipient a minute apart look exactly like one send and one retry.

The third instinct is to make the operation naturally idempotent. That works beautifully for a database upsert and fails completely for anything that leaves your control. A document that has been transmitted cannot be un-transmitted by a second call.

An idempotency key, generated by the caller, attached to the request, and honoured by the

The contract is simple. The caller invents a unique string per logical operation. Every attempt

carries the same string. The first request to arrive does the work and records the result against that key. Every later request carrying that string gets the recorded result back instead of doing the work again.

The important part is where the state lives. It is on the server, which is the only place that knows

whether the job exists. Your retry loop stops needing to be clever. It just needs to reuse the key.

Stripe popularised this pattern and the reason it spread is that the alternative is unbounded.

Without it, every caller writes their own deduplication, badly, and the failure only shows up under load.

Document and messaging APIs are where it matters most, because the side effect is visible to

someone outside your company. Fax for Agents is a current example of the pattern taken

seriously. It requires an Idempotency-Key on every mutating call, so a retry hands back the

same job instead of creating a second one.

Idempotency solves the duplicate. It does not solve the other half, which is that you still do not know what happened.

Most integrations model this as a boolean. Sent, or not sent. That is wrong, and you discover it

the first time a transmission half completes.

A useful state machine for a send has more room in it. Fax for Agents publishes six states:

queued, active, partial, failed, unknown and delivered. Two of those are the interesting ones.

unknown means the provider has not said. Not failed, not delivered. The correct response to

unknown is to wait. A retry loop that treats unknown as failure needs rewriting, because that is

how you produce duplicates even with idempotency in place.

partial means some of it arrived. Resending the whole document would deliver the first pages

twice, so partial is a state that must never be retransmitted automatically. It needs a person, or a

And delivered should be the narrowest word in the vocabulary. In that API it is written only when

the provider's evidence for the attempt supports it, never when the request was merely

accepted. The gap between "we accepted your job" and "it arrived" is where most false

Four questions, answerable from the documentation before you write a line of code.

Does it accept a caller-supplied idempotency string on every mutating call, and for how long

How many terminal states does a job have? If the answer is two, the API is hiding something

Is there a state meaning "we do not know yet", and does the documentation say what you should do when you see it?

When a webhook arrives, can you verify it came from them? Signed delivery with an event id is

the difference between a webhook handler and an open endpoint.

None of that is exotic. It is the difference between an integration that degrades and one that

#10892. DNA robots could deliver drugs and hunt viruses inside your body

Imagine tiny robots made from DNA moving through the bloodstream, delivering drugs exactly where they are needed and targeting threats such as cancer cells or viruses. These microscopic machines could also assemble ultra-precise data storage systems and computing devices at the nanometer scale. Although the possibilities are remarkable, most DNA robots today remain in early experimental stages and are better understood as proof of concept rather than practical tools.

Researchers are exploring how DNA can be engineered into working machines using creative design approaches. These include building rigid DNA joints, incorporating flexible components, and using folding techniques inspired by origami. By applying principles from larger-scale robotics such as rigid, compliant and origami robots, scientists are adapting familiar mechanical concepts to the nanoscale. This allows DNA-based systems to carry out controlled and repeatable tasks despite their extremely small size.

Guiding the motion of DNA robots in a constantly shifting molecular environment is a major challenge. To address this, scientists have developed control systems that help these machines behave in predictable ways. One important method involves DNA strand displacement, a biochemical process that enables precise programming of movement using specific DNA sequences labeled as "fuel" and "structure."

In addition to biochemical control, external physical signals such as electric fields, magnetic fields, and light can direct how these robots move. Together, these approaches provide a toolkit for fine-tuning the behavior of DNA machines with a high degree of accuracy.

The potential uses for DNA robots extend well beyond laboratory experiments. In medicine, they could function as "nano-surgeons," locating diseased cells and delivering targeted treatments with precision. Researchers are also exploring whether these machines could capture viruses like SARS-CoV-2, with future systems potentially operating as fully autonomous drug delivery platforms.

DNA robots may also play a role in advanced manufacturing. Acting as programmable templates, they could position nanoparticles with sub-nanometer accuracy. This capability could lead to breakthroughs in molecular computing and highly efficient optical devices that outperform current technologies.

Despite rapid progress, several obstacles remain. Moving from large-scale systems to molecular machines introduces challenges such as Brownian motion, which makes precise control more difficult. Many current DNA robot designs are still relatively simple and operate in isolation, limiting their usefulness in complex real-world environments.

There are also gaps in foundational knowledge. Researchers still lack detailed databases describing the mechanical properties of DNA structures, and simulation tools for predicting behavior at this scale are not yet fully developed.

To overcome these barriers, scientists emphasize the need for collaboration across disciplines. Proposed solutions include creating standardized DNA "parts libraries," using artificial intelligence to improve design and simulation, and advancing bio-manufacturing methods. Progress in these areas will be essential for scaling DNA robots and integrating them into practical applications in healthcare, manufacturing, and beyond.

"The robots of tomorrow won't just be made of metal and plastic," says the research team. "They will be biological, programmable, and intelligent. They will be the tools that allow us to finally master the molecular world."

Materials provided by Journal Center of Harbin Institute of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10887. How to Build an AI-Assisted Workflow That Does Not Leak

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A generative AI tool can draft a client email, summarise a forty-message thread or refactor a function in seconds. That speed is real, and it explains how fast these tools have become part of the ordinary working day, used to summarise threads, draft replies and reformat data in a single paste. The same ease is the problem. It moves sensitive material into systems that were never scoped to hold it, quietly and at scale.

Here is the uncomfortable part for anyone running a lean team. The risk in that workflow is almost never the model being wrong. It is that nobody decided what should go into the tool in the first place, and nobody is accountable for where the data ends up. AI made the drafting instant. It did nothing on its own to make delegation safe.

This article is about the control that closes that gap. It is not a product and it is not a policy document. It is a person, placed deliberately in the workflow, making the one call that no model can make for you.

Samsung learned this in public. In 2023, engineers in its semiconductor division were given access to ChatGPT to help debug source code. Within weeks, staff had pasted proprietary code and internal meeting notes into the tool, and Samsung banned generative AI across the company soon after. The story ran everywhere, from Bloomberg to The Register. Nothing malfunctioned. People simply moved sensitive material into a system that was never scoped to hold it, and the material was gone.

Two things make this failure mode easy to hit, and both are structural rather than careless.

First, most of these tools run in the browser. The data loss prevention software a security team relies on was built to watch files, endpoints and network egress, and it often cannot see a paste action into a web app at all. Worse, much of this activity happens through personal accounts that sit outside the controls the organisation does have. The leak does not trip an alarm because nothing was downloaded and nothing was emailed. Text was typed into a box.

Second, consumer-grade accounts can retain what you give them. OpenAI's own documentation states that content from consumer ChatGPT may be used to improve its models unless the user turns that setting off. Paste a client list or a contract clause from the wrong account, and that material may now sit inside a training pipeline you do not control and cannot claw back.

For a developer or a founder, the lesson is not "do not use AI". It is that the point of failure sits one layer above the model, in how the work is handed off and who decided what was safe to hand over.

If any of the data moving through that workflow identifies a person, a client, a candidate, an employee or an investor, then GDPR treats the tools and people touching it as processors acting on your behalf. That sounds abstract until you translate it into design constraints, which is what a technical team can actually work with.

Under Article 28 of the GDPR, anyone who processes personal data for you must do so under a binding contract that sets out what is processed, for what purpose and for how long, and everyone handling that data must be under a confidentiality commitment. An AI vendor you have never papered is a processor you have not accounted for. A free account used quietly by one engineer is a sub-processor nobody signed off. The obligation does not disappear because the tool was convenient.

Location is the second constraint, and it is the one most teams miss. A person, contractor or tool processing your data outside the EU means personal data is being transferred outside the EU, which requires a specific safeguard such as the Standard Contractual Clauses on top of the Article 28 contract. Keep the procesing inside the EU and that entire category of transfer risk falls away before you have to manage it. For UK teams the position is the same: the Information Commissioner's Office sets out the identical core terms for a processor contract, documented instructions, confidentiality, security measures, rules on sub-processors, and deletion or return of data at the end of the engagement.

There is now a second regulatory layer as well. The EU AI Act, which entered into force in 2024, adds obligations on how AI systems are deployed and governed, on top of the data-protection duties GDPR already imposes. The detail is still settling, but the direction is clear: unmanaged, undocumented AI use is getting harder to defend, not easier. None of this is exotic. It is the same governance you would apply to any third party with access to your systems. AI simply introduced a new processor that most workflows never wrote down.

"Human in the loop" usually gets discussed as a quality measure: a person checks the output so errors do not ship. That is true, and it undersells the point badly. The human is also the only part of the workflow that can make a judgment about what should never enter the tool at all.

A model cannot decide that a board deck stays out of a consumer chatbot. A browser extension cannot know that one folder holds client data governed by a contract that forbids sub-processing without written consent. Those are judgment calls, and judgment is precisely the thing you cannot automate. The person doing the delegating is your policy enforcement point, sitting at the exact moment the decision is made. They decide what gets pasted, what gets redacted first, what runs only inside an enterprise account with training switched off, and what a human handles end to end because the risk is simply not worth the minutes saved.

This reframes the division of labour that actually holds up. The AI owns the mechanical layer: summarising a long thread, turning rough notes into a first draft, extracting action items, reformatting data. The human owns the judgment layer: what matters, how it is framed, and, above all, where the data is allowed to go. Remove the human and you have not automated the work. You have removed the only control that was reading the situation and deciding what was safe.

It is worth being specific about what that judgment looks like in practice, because it is a skill and not a job title. Someone exercising it knows which inputs are off limits without having to ask, keeps sensitive material inside controlled systems by default, and treats the question "should this go into the tool at all" as part of the work rather than an afterthought. Whether that person is an engineer, an operations lead or an executive assistant, the control is the same: someone with enough context to know what is sensitive, and enough standing to say no. That habit is what separates a workflow that is genuinely governed from one that merely has a policy document nobody reads.

The practical version, for a founder or a small technical team that wants the speed without the exposure:

Most teams can put the first four in place in about a week. The last two are harder, because they are not configuration changes. They are about whether the workflow still holds when the people in it change, and that is the part most teams discover they got wrong only after someone has already left.

The speed of AI is not in question. What AI cannot supply is the judgment about what should enter it, and the accountability for where the output goes. Treat the human in the loop as a security control, design the workflow around that control, and you keep the nine-second draft without the quiet data leak. Remove the human, and the only thing you have really automated is the leak.

#10886. AI breakthrough cuts energy use by 100x while boosting accuracy

Artificial intelligence is consuming enormous amounts of electricity in the United States. According to the International Energy Agency, AI systems and data centers used about 415 terawatt hours of power in 2024. That accounts for more than 10% of the country's total electricity production, and demand is projected to double by 2030.

This rapid growth has raised concerns about sustainability. In response, researchers at a School of Engineering have created a proof-of-concept AI system designed to be far more efficient. Their approach could reduce energy use by up to 100 times while also improving performance on tasks.

A Hybrid Approach Called Neuro-Symbolic AI

The research comes from the laboratory of Matthias Scheutz, Karol Family Applied Technology Professor. His team is developing neuro-symbolic AI, which combines traditional neural networks with symbolic reasoning. This method mirrors how people approach problems by breaking them into steps and categories.

The work will be presented at the International Conference of Robotics and Automation in Vienna in May and will appear in the conference proceedings.

Teaching Robots to See, Understand, and Act

Unlike familiar large language models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT and Gemini, the team focuses on AI systems used in robotics. These systems are known as visual-language-action (VLA) models. They extend LLM capabilities by incorporating vision and physical movement.

VLA models take in visual data from cameras and instructions from language, then translate that information into real-world actions. For example, they can control a robot's wheels, arms, or fingers to complete a task.

Why Traditional AI Struggles With Simple Tasks

Conventional VLA systems rely heavily on data and trial-and-error learning. If a robot is asked to stack blocks into a tower, it must first analyze the scene, identify each block, and determine how to place them correctly.

This process often leads to mistakes. Shadows may confuse the system about a block's shape, or the robot may place pieces incorrectly, causing the structure to collapse.

These errors are similar to the problems seen in LLMs. Just as robots can misplace blocks, chatbots can generate false or misleading outputs. Examples include fabricating legal cases or producing images with unrealistic details such as extra fingers.

How Symbolic Reasoning Improves Accuracy and Efficiency

Symbolic reasoning offers a different strategy. Instead of relying only on patterns from data, it uses rules and abstract concepts such as shape and balance. This allows the system to plan more effectively and avoid unnecessary trial and error.

"Like an LLM, VLA models act on statistical results from large training sets of similar scenarios, but that can lead to errors," said Scheutz. "A neuro-symbolic VLA can apply rules that limit the amount of trial and error during learning and get to a solution much faster. Not only does it complete the task much faster, but the time spent on training the system is significantly reduced."

The researchers tested their system using the Tower of Hanoi puzzle, a classic problem that requires careful planning.

The neuro-symbolic VLA achieved a 95% success rate, compared with just 34% for standard systems. When given a more complex version of the puzzle that it had not encountered before, the hybrid system still succeeded 78% of the time. Traditional models failed every attempt.

Training time also dropped sharply. The new system learned the task in only 34 minutes, while conventional models required more than a day and a half.

Massive Energy Savings in Training and Use

Energy consumption was reduced dramatically as well. Training the neuro-symbolic model required only 1% of the energy used by a standard VLA system. During operation, it used just 5% of the energy needed by conventional approaches.

Scheutz compared this inefficiency to everyday AI tools. "These systems are just trying to predict the next word or action in a sequence, but that can be imperfect, and they can come up with inaccurate results or hallucinations. Their energy expense is often disproportionate to the task. For example, when you search on Google, the AI summary at the top of the page consumes up to 100 times more energy than the generation of the website listings."

The Growing Strain of AI on Power Infrastructure

As AI adoption accelerates across industries, demand for computing power continues to climb. Companies are building increasingly large data centers, some of which require hundreds of megawatts of electricity. That level of consumption can exceed the needs of entire small cities.

This trend has sparked a race to expand infrastructure, raising concerns about long-term energy limits.

The researchers suggest that current approaches based on LLMs and VLAs may not be sustainable in the long run. While these systems are powerful, they consume large amounts of energy and can still produce unreliable results.

In contrast, neuro-symbolic AI offers a different direction. By combining learning with structured reasoning, it may provide a more efficient and dependable foundation for future AI systems.

Materials provided by Tufts University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10885. Anthropic mathematician claims to have solved 167-year-old math problem with Claude

"I believe I have, working with Claude, discovered a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In the same week that Anthropic was floated on the Nasdaq in its initial public offering, a mathematician employed by the company claims to have discovered proof behind one of the world's most famous math problem, the 167-year-old Riemann hypothesis. Levent Alpöge wrote in a paper this week, "I believe I have, working with Claude, discovered a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis."

As you're no doubt already aware, and as Alpöge explains, the Riemann Hypothesis is the classic problem in number theory and one of the most famous unsolved problems in mathematics. Proposed in 1859 by Bernhard Riemann, who gave the problem its name, it is the theory that prime numbers are not in fact random but determined. "The Riemann hypothesis asserts that all interesting solutions of the equation ζ(s) = 0 lie on a certain vertical straight line," the Clay Mathematics Institute explains. "This has been checked for the first 10,000,000,000,000 solutions. A proof that it is true for every interesting solution would shed light on many of the mysteries surrounding the distribution of prime numbers."

As Scientific American notes, the problem is the "North Star for the entire field of number theory, and the most famous open problem in math." Furthermore, the problem was previously thought so insurmountable that experts didn't even bother working on it.

Alpöge not only believes that he has used Claude to discover a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis, but also believes the method could easily extend to the so-called Generalized Riemann Hypothesis and the Extended Riemann Hypothesis, too. "An astounding amount of mathematics is present in the argument, far more than I had imagined would be necessary as a student," he writes.

This would not be the first time that AI has made a significant discovery or finding in a major STEM field. Earlier this year, OpenAI helped create a breakthrough solution for the Navier-Stokes problem, a finding somewhat overshadowed by a plagiarism controversy.

Alpöge's statement is not without reservation. Aside from confirming whether Claude has actually helped crack the Riemann Hypothesis, which will take some time, he admits sadness at the finding. "It is natural to feel that this is an end, in my case a loss of a principal guiding light, but what I saw during the course of progress so amazed me that I believe it is one dawn across science of something far greater."

"In a time of revolution in mathematics, I hope the argument provides a glimpse towards the miracles that lie ahead," Alpöge adds.

#10881. How to Learn Java with AI: A Practical Guide for Beginners

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Java has been one of the most in-demand programming languages for over two decades. It powers Android apps, banking systems, enterprise software, and huge backend platforms. The good news: learning it has never been easier. With AI tools like Claude, ChatGPT, and GitHub Copilot, you now have a patient tutor available at any hour.

The catch is that AI can help you learn faster or help you avoid learning at all. This guide shows you how to use it the right way.

Traditionally, learning Java meant reading a textbook, watching videos, and getting stuck on a compiler error for two hours with nobody to ask. AI removes much of that friction:

Instant explanations. Ask "what is the difference between an abstract class and an interface?" and get an answer pitched at your level.

Personalized pace. Ask for the same concept explained three different ways until it clicks.

Error decoding. Paste a confusing stack trace and get a plain-English explanation of what went wrong.

Unlimited practice. Generate fresh exercises on any topic, any time.

But AI is a tutor, not a replacement for practice. You still have to write the code yourself.

Before writing code, you need the JDK (Java Development Kit) and an IDE such as IntelliJ IDEA, Eclipse, or VS Code.

"I'm a complete beginner on Windows. Walk me through installing the JDK and IntelliJ IDEA, and help me run my first Hello World program."

If something goes wrong (and it often does with PATH variables), paste the exact error message and ask what it means.

Use AI to build understanding with analogies and examples.

"Explain Java loops like I'm a beginner. Give me one real-world analogy, then three short examples that get progressively harder."

Then close the chat and write your own loop from memory. That second step is where learning actually happens.

OOP is where many beginners struggle, and where AI shines as a tutor. Concepts like classes, objects, inheritance, polymorphism, encapsulation, and abstraction are easier to grasp when you can ask follow-up questions.

"Teach me inheritance in Java using a Vehicle, Car, and Bike example. Then quiz me with 5 questions, one at a time, and wait for my answer before giving feedback."

Asking the AI to quiz you, rather than just explain, turns passive reading into active recall.

Once you've written a program, ask AI to review it. This is one of the most valuable habits you can build.

"Review my Java code. Point out bugs, style issues, and anything that isn't idiomatic. Explain why each suggestion matters, but don't rewrite the whole thing for me."

That last sentence is important. You want feedback and understanding, not a finished solution you copy and paste.

When your code throws a NullPointerException or ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException, resist the urge to say "fix this." Instead, try:

"Here's my code and the error. Don't give me the answer yet. Ask me guiding questions so I can find the bug myself."

Debugging is a core professional skill. Using AI as a Socratic coach builds that skill instead of bypassing it.

Tutorials only take you so far. Projects are where Java sticks. Ask AI to suggest projects matched to your level:

Beginner: Number guessing game, to-do list in the console, simple calculator

Intermediate: Library management system, bank account simulator, student record manager

Advanced: REST API with Spring Boot, inventory system with a database, a chat application

"I know Java basics and OOP. Suggest a project I can finish in a weekend, break it into milestones, and don't write the code for me."

Copy-pasting without understanding. If you can't explain a line of code, you haven't learned it. Ask the AI to explain it line by line.

Trusting AI blindly. AI can produce code that looks right but is wrong, outdated, or insecure. Always run it, test it, and verify against official documentation.

Skipping the struggle. A little frustration is productive. Try for 15 to 20 minutes before asking for help.

Never writing code from scratch. Regularly practice without AI so you know what you can actually do on your own, especially before job interviews.

AI is a fantastic assistant, but it can't tell you what you don't know you need to learn. A structured course gives you a proven roadmap, so you cover the right topics in the right order, while AI fills in the gaps, answers your questions, and keeps you moving.

The best way to learn Java with AI is to treat it like a good mentor: ask it to explain, challenge, and review, but do the typing yourself. Learners who combine consistent practice with smart AI use progress faster than ever before.

#10880. This new chip survives 1300°F (700°C) and could change AI forever

Modern electronics power everything from smartphones to satellites, but they all share a major limitation. Heat. Once temperatures climb above roughly 200 degrees Celsius, most devices begin to break down. For decades, this thermal barrier has been one of the toughest challenges in engineering.

Researchers at the University of Southern California now believe they have found a way past that limit.

In a study published on March 26, 2026 in Science, a team led by Joshua Yang, Arthur B. Freeman Chair Professor at the Ming Hsieh Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the USC Viterbi School of Engineering and the USC School of Advanced Computing, unveiled a new type of memory device that continues to operate at 700 degrees Celsius (~1300 degrees Fahrenheit). That temperature exceeds molten lava and goes far beyond anything previously achieved for this class of technology. The device showed no sign of failure. In fact, 700 degrees was simply the maximum their equipment could test.

"You may call it a revolution," Yang said. "It is the best high-temperature memory ever demonstrated."

The new device is known as a memristor, a nanoscale component that can both store data and perform computations. It is constructed like a microscopic layered structure, with two electrodes on either side and a thin ceramic layer in between.

Jian Zhao, the study's first author, built the device using tungsten for the top electrode, hafnium oxide ceramic in the middle, and graphene for the bottom layer. Tungsten has the highest melting point of any element, while graphene, a single-atom-thick sheet of carbon, is known for its exceptional strength and heat resistance.

This combination produced remarkable performance. The device retained data for more than 50 hours at 700 degrees without needing to be refreshed. It also endured over one billion switching cycles at that temperature and operated at just 1.5 volts with speeds measured in tens of nanoseconds.

The discovery was not part of the team's original plan. They were initially attempting to create a different graphene-based device, which did not work as intended. Along the way, they encountered something surprising.

"To be honest, it was by accident, as most discoveries are," Yang said. "If you can predict it, it's usually not surprising, and probably not significant enough."

Further investigation revealed why the device performed so well. In conventional electronics, heat causes metal atoms in the top electrode to slowly migrate through the ceramic layer. Eventually, they reach the bottom electrode, creating a permanent connection that short-circuits the device and leaves it stuck in the on state.

Graphene prevents this failure. Its interaction with tungsten is, as Yang described it, similar to oil and water. Tungsten atoms that approach the graphene surface cannot attach to it. Without a stable point to settle, they drift away instead of forming a conductive bridge. This prevents short circuits and preserves the device's function even under extreme heat.

The researchers confirmed this mechanism using advanced electron microscopy, spectroscopy, and quantum-level simulations. By understanding what happens at the atomic interface, they have turned an unexpected result into a principle that could guide future designs. Other materials with similar surface properties could be identified, which may help scale the technology for industrial production.

Electronics capable of operating above 500 degrees Celsius have long been a goal for space exploration. Venus, for example, has a surface temperature around that level, and every lander sent there has failed in part due to extreme heat. Current silicon-based chips cannot survive such conditions.

"We are now above 700 degrees, and we suspect it will go higher," Yang said.

The potential applications go far beyond space missions. Geothermal energy systems require electronics that can function deep underground, where surrounding rock can glow red-hot. Nuclear and fusion systems also expose equipment to intense heat. Even in everyday settings, durability improves significantly. A device rated for 700 degrees would be extremely robust at the roughly 125-degree temperatures often reached inside automotive electronics.

Why It Matters for Artificial Intelligence

In addition to storing data, the device offers a major advantage for artificial intelligence. Many AI systems rely heavily on matrix multiplication, a mathematical operation used in tasks like image recognition and language processing. Traditional computers perform these calculations step by step, consuming large amounts of energy.

Memristors approach the problem differently. By using Ohm's Law, where voltage times conductance equals current, the device performs calculations directly as electricity flows through it. The result is obtained instantly as the measured current.

"Over 92 percent of the computing in AI systems like ChatGPT is nothing but matrix multiplication," Yang said. "This type of device can perform that in the most efficient way, orders of magnitude faster and at lower energy."

Yang and three co-authors of the study (Qiangfei Xia, Miao Hu, and Ning Ge) have already co-founded a company called TetraMem to commercialize memristor-based AI chips at room temperature. Their lab is already using working chips from TetraMem for machine learning tasks. The high-temperature version described in this research could extend those capabilities to environments where traditional electronics cannot operate, allowing devices such as spacecraft or industrial sensors to process data directly on site.

From Lab Prototype to Real-World Technology

Despite the promising results, Yang emphasizes that practical applications are still some distance away. Memory is only one part of a complete computing system. High-temperature logic circuits will also need to be developed and integrated. In addition, the current devices were built manually at very small scales in a laboratory setting, so manufacturing at scale will take time.

"This is the first step," Yang said. "It's still a long way to go. But logically, you can see: now it makes it possible. The missing component has been made."

From a manufacturing perspective, two of the materials used in the device, tungsten and hafnium oxide, are already widely used in semiconductor production. Graphene is newer but is actively being developed by major companies such as TSMC and Samsung, and it has already been produced at wafer scale in research environments.

The work was conducted through the CONCRETE Center, short for Center of Neuromorphic Computing under Extreme Environments, a multi-university Center of Excellence led by USC and supported by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research and the Air Force Research Laboratory. Key experimental work was carried out in collaboration with Dr. Sabyasachi Ganguli's team at the AFRL Materials Lab in Dayton, Ohio, while theoretical analysis involved USC researchers and collaborators at Kumamoto University in Japan.

For Yang, publication in Science reflects more than a single achievement.

"Space exploration has never been so real, so close, and at such a large scale," he said. "This paper represents a critical leap into a much larger, more exciting frontier."

Materials provided by University of Southern California. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10874. How to compete in the $100,000 November Fortnite PlayStation Cup

The cup qualifiers start on November 23, with separate competitions for Battle Royale and Zero Build. Both are Solo events, so you can jump in without having to find a duo partner or put a squad together.

Here’s how to enter the Fortnite PlayStation Cup on your PlayStation, when each round takes place, and what you can win.

Before the Cup starts, make sure your PlayStation and Epic Games accounts are linked and that you’ve enabled two-factor authentication. Your Epic account needs to be in good standing and at least level 50, which you can check in the Career tab. You’ll also need to be at least 13 and meet the eligibility requirements for your country.

The Cup starts on Monday, November 23, with qualifiers for both Battle Royale and Zero Build. Top 80 players who advance in Zero Build will compete in the finals on Tuesday, November 24, while the Battle Royale finals take place on Wednesday, November 25.

This gives you just one day to book a spot in the finals, so you’ll have to rack up those wins as quickly as possible.

Each mode has $50,000 in prize money split across seven regions. In Europe, the top 50 finishers in each final earn cash, with $4,000 going to first place. North America Central also pays out to the top 50, with $2,500 for the winner.

In North America West, Brazil, the Middle East, Oceania, and Asia, the top 25 players in each final earn a cash prize, with $750 for first place.

#10873. Top 4 MCP Servers for Marketers: Features, Use Cases, and Limitations

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You no longer need to build a dashboard every time you want to investigate a drop in traffic or compare campaign results. With servers built on the Model Context Protocol (MCP), you can ask an AI assistant a question and let it retrieve the relevant data directly from your marketing tools.

But connecting an assistant to Google Analytics is not the same as connecting it to HubSpot or Ahrefs. Each server exposes different data, capabilities and levels of access. Some are built for quick analysis inside one platform, while others support reporting across several channels. This guide compares four servers, what they can do, how they are set up and where their limits matter. Details such as plans and tool lists change often, so confirm them against each vendor's documentation before you commit.

The summary below reflects each vendor's documentation at the time of writing.

Google publishes an official, open-source Google Analytics MCP server for GA4. It connects AI assistants to the Admin and Data APIs, so it can list accounts and properties, run standard, realtime, funnel and conversion reports, and inspect custom dimensions and metrics. That makes it useful for investigating traffic changes, landing page performance and engagement trends.

Example prompt: "Which landing pages lost the most engaged sessions this month compared with last month?" The assistant can pull the relevant reports and help you spot changes, and you can then check whether the decline comes from specific channels, countries or devices.

Setup: The server runs locally. Google's documentation lists Python 3.10 or later, pipx or uvx, a Google Cloud project with the Analytics Admin and Data APIs enabled, application default credentials with the read-only Analytics scope, and at least Viewer access on the property you want to query.

Access: Google describes the server as available for read requests only, so it cannot change your Analytics configuration.

Limitations: It covers GA4 only, so it cannot combine website metrics with CRM records or advertising costs on its own. Running it locally also means you maintain the configuration yourself.

Best for: Web analysts and technical marketers who regularly investigate GA4 performance.

Marketing performance usually spans several platforms. Google Ads generates clicks, GA4 tracks site activity and a CRM records leads. Seeing the whole picture means bringing those metrics together.

Coupler is a no-code data integration platform that offers MCP access to prepared business data. It lists more than 400 supported data sources, including Google Ads, Facebook Ads, GA4, HubSpot, LinkedIn Ads and Shopify, and lets teams combine and transform data before an assistant queries it.

Example prompt: "What was our cost per lead across Google Ads, Meta Ads and LinkedIn last quarter?" For paid search specifically, Coulper describes a Google Ads MCP server that lets you ask about campaigns and compare them with other connected channels.

Setup: Coupler is hosted, so there is nothing to install locally. You connect your platforms, configure data flows, choose refresh schedules and connect a compatible AI assistant. According to the vendor, queries run against the prepared datasets, which is useful for recurring reports because teams can reuse refreshed data instead of preparing exports by hand.

Best for: Agencies and teams that need consistent reporting across advertising, website analytics, ecommerce and CRM platforms.

HubSpot's remote MCP server became generally available on April 13, 2026. It connects compatible AI clients to CRM data such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities, and can also read marketing objects such as campaigns, landing pages and blog posts. That helps marketers investigate lead quality, pipeline performance and lifecycle trends without reviewing records one by one.

Example prompt: "Which deals created last quarter originated from webinar leads, and how many closed?" The assistant can retrieve the records and examine conversion patterns, provided the attribution data exists.

Setup: HubSpot hosts the server. In your account you create an MCP auth app under Development, then connect an MCP client that supports OAuth 2.1 with PKCE to the HubSpot endpoint. See HubSpot's remote MCP server documentation for current steps.

Access: Unlike a purely analytical server, HubSpot supports selected create and update actions on records such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities. Other objects, including marketing content, are read-only.

Limitations: Available operations differ by object type, and incomplete lead sources or inconsistent lifecycle stages will weaken any analysis.

Best for: B2B marketers and revenue operations teams that manage customers and pipeline in HubSpot.

Ahrefs offers a hosted MCP server that brings its SEO data into compatible assistants. It supports conversational research into keywords, backlinks, competing pages and organic visibility, so you can follow up with questions instead of exporting report after report.

Example prompt: "Which websites link to both of our main competitors but don't link to us?" The assistant can retrieve backlink data and help you identify outreach targets, which you can then narrow by Domain Rating or organic traffic.

Setup: According to the Ahrefs MCP documentation, the remote server is available on paid plans starting from Lite, and you authorize it through your AI client. Ahrefs also publishes a getting started guide.

Limitations: Calls made through MCP use your monthly API units in the same way as direct API usage, and rows per request depend on your plan. Ahrefs documents a default cap of 200,000 units per month for keys created through OAuth, which workspace admins can change. The data is also SEO-focused rather than first-party conversion analytics.

Best for: SEO specialists, content marketers and link builders who already use Ahrefs.

Yes, but start with the connections your team actually needs. Each additional server adds tools the assistant has to choose between, which increases complexity. Combining Ahrefs and HubSpot, for example, supports separate SEO and CRM investigations, but connecting several servers does not automatically create a reliable cross-platform reporting system.

For recurring reports, prepare consistent datasets and validate the first results against your existing dashboards. That catches errors caused by mismatched metrics or attribution rules.

Are MCP servers safe for marketing data? Security depends on the provider, the authentication method and the permissions you grant. Use trusted servers, review access scopes, and enable write operations only when necessary.

Do I need coding skills to use MCP servers? Not necessarily. Hosted services usually need less configuration, while local servers such as Google's need extra setup.

Which AI clients support MCP? Claude, ChatGPT, Cursor and other tools support MCP connections, though functionality and setup differ.

Do MCP servers cost extra? It depends on the provider. Some require paid plans, and others consume API credits or have usage limits.

Pick the server that matches the question you ask most often: Google Analytics MCP for GA4 behavior, Coupler for cross-channel reporting, HubSpot for CRM and lifecycle analysis, and Ahrefs for SEO research. Start with read-only access, check usage limits and permissions, and validate early answers against the reports you already trust.

#10872. This new chip could slash data center energy waste

As data centers consume more energy to support growing digital demands, engineers at the University of California San Diego have introduced a new chip design that could make powering graphics processing units (GPUs) more efficient. The innovation focuses on a key function in electronics: converting high voltages into the lower levels required by computing hardware. In laboratory testing, a prototype chip successfully performed this type of voltage conversion with high efficiency under conditions similar to those found in modern data centers.

The findings, published in Nature Communications, suggest the potential for smaller and more energy-efficient systems in advanced computing environments.

Rethinking DC-DC Converters for Modern Electronics

At the center of the new design is an improved version of a widely used component known as a DC-DC step-down converter. These converters are found in nearly all electronic devices and serve as a critical link between power sources and sensitive circuits. Their job is to take a high incoming voltage and reduce it to the exact level needed for safe operation.

In data centers, electricity is often distributed at 48 volts, while GPU processors typically require much lower voltages, usually between 1 and 5 volts. Efficiently managing this large voltage drop has become increasingly challenging as computing systems grow more powerful and compact.

Limits of Traditional Power Conversion Technology

Conventional step-down converters often struggle when dealing with large differences between input and output voltage. As that gap increases, efficiency drops and it becomes harder to supply enough current. Most existing designs rely on magnetic components such as inductors. While these components have been refined over many years, they are approaching their practical limits and are becoming harder to improve further.

"We've gotten so good at designing inductive converters that there's not really much room left to improve them to meet future needs," said study senior author Patrick Mercier, professor in the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the UC San Diego Jacobs School of Engineering.

Exploring Piezoelectric Resonators as an Alternative

To move beyond these limitations, Mercier and his team, including first author Jae-Young Ko, an electrical and computer engineering Ph.D. student at UC San Diego, investigated a different approach using piezoelectric resonators. These small devices store and transfer energy through mechanical vibrations rather than magnetic fields.

Converters based on piezoelectric components could offer several advantages. They have the potential to be smaller, more energy dense, more efficient, and easier to manufacture at scale. "They have a lot of room to grow and have the potential to deliver better performance than anything that's come before them," Mercier said.

However, earlier versions of piezoelectric converters have had difficulty maintaining efficiency and delivering sufficient power when handling large voltage differences.

Hybrid Design Achieves High Efficiency and Power Output

To overcome these issues, the researchers created a hybrid converter that combines a piezoelectric resonator with small, commercially available capacitors arranged in a carefully designed configuration. This setup enables the system to handle larger voltage conversions more effectively.

The team incorporated this design into a prototype chip and tested its performance. The device successfully converted 48 volts down to 4.8 volts -- a level commonly required in data centers -- with a peak efficiency of 96.2 percent. It also delivered roughly four times more output current than previous piezoelectric-based designs.

This hybrid approach offers several benefits. It creates multiple pathways for energy to move through the system, reduces wasted power, and lessens the strain on the resonator. Together, these improvements enhance both efficiency and power delivery while only slightly increasing the chip's size.

Challenges and Next Steps for Real-World Use

Although the technology shows strong promise, it is still in the early stages of development. The researchers view it as an important step toward overcoming the constraints of current power conversion systems. Future efforts will focus on refining materials, improving circuit designs, and developing better packaging methods.

One challenge is that piezoelectric resonators physically vibrate, which means they cannot be attached to circuit boards using standard soldering techniques. New integration strategies will be needed to incorporate them into electronic systems, Mercier explained.

"Piezoelectric-based converters aren't quite ready to replace existing power converter technologies yet," Mercier added. "But they offer a trajectory for improvement. We need to continue to improve on multiple areas -- materials, circuits and packaging -- to make this technology ready for data center applications."

This project was supported in part by the Power Management Integration Center (PMIC), an Industry-University Cooperative Research Center (IUCRC) funded by the National Science Foundation (award number 2052809).

Materials provided by University of California - San Diego. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10868. “Giant superatoms” could finally solve quantum computing’s biggest problem

Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have introduced a new theoretical design for quantum systems based on what they call "giant superatoms." This concept offers a fresh way to protect, control, and share quantum information, potentially bringing scientists closer to building large-scale quantum computers.

Quantum computers are expected to transform fields like drug discovery and encryption by solving problems that are far beyond the reach of conventional machines. However, progress has been limited by a major challenge known as decoherence. This occurs when quantum bits, or qubits, lose their information due to interactions with their surroundings. Even small amounts of electromagnetic noise can disrupt the fragile quantum states needed for computation.

"Quantum systems are extraordinarily powerful but also extremely fragile. The key to making them useful is learning how to control their interaction with the surrounding environment," says Lei Du, postdoctoral researcher in applied quantum technology at Chalmers.

Lei Du is the lead author of a study that outlines this new type of quantum system. The design is built around giant superatoms, which combine several important features. These systems reduce decoherence, remain stable, and consist of multiple interconnected "atoms" that function together as a single unit.

Giant superatoms bring together two previously separate ideas in quantum physics: giant atoms and superatoms. While each has been studied on its own, this is the first time they have been merged into a single system. These structures behave like atoms but are not found in nature. Instead, they are engineered by scientists (see fact box below).

The idea of giant atoms was first introduced by researchers at Chalmers over a decade ago and is now widely used in the field. A giant atom is typically designed as a qubit (which is the smallest unit of quantum information). Unlike ordinary atoms, it connects to light or sound waves at multiple, physically separated points. This allows it to interact with its environment in several places at once, helping it preserve quantum information.

"Waves that leave one connection point can travel through the environment and return to affect the atom at another point -- similar to hearing an echo of your own voice before you've finished speaking. This self-interaction leads to highly beneficial quantum effects, reduces decoherence and gives the system a form of memory of past interactions," explains Anton Frisk Kockum, Associate Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Although giant atoms have improved understanding of quantum behavior, they have had limitations when it comes to entanglement. Entanglement allows multiple qubits to share a single quantum state and act as one coordinated system, which is essential for powerful quantum computers.

To overcome this limitation, the research team combined giant atoms with the concept of superatoms. A superatom consists of several natural atoms that share the same quantum state and behave collectively as one larger atom.

This combination is expected to make it easier to create complex quantum states needed for quantum communication, networks, and highly sensitive measurement systems.

"A giant superatom may be envisaged as multiple giant atoms working together as a single entity, exhibiting a non-local interaction between light and matter. This enables quantum information from multiple qubits to be stored and controlled within one unit, without the need for increasingly complex surrounding circuitry," explains Lei Du.

"Giant superatoms open the door to entirely new capabilities, giving us a powerful new toolbox. They allow us to control quantum information and create entanglement in ways that were previously extremely difficult, or even impossible," says Janine Splettstoesser, Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Toward Scalable and Practical Quantum Systems

This work creates new possibilities for building quantum systems that are both scalable and reliable. The researchers plan to move from theory toward actually constructing these systems. Their design could also be integrated with other quantum technologies, serving as a building block for connecting different types of quantum platforms.

"There is currently strong interest in hybrid approaches, in which different quantum systems work together, because each has its own strengths," says Anton Frisk Kockum. "Our research shows that smart design can reduce the need for increasingly complex hardware and giant superatoms are bringing us one step closer to practically applicable quantum technology."

More on: Methods for protecting, controlling and distributing quantum information

The study shows that the way giant superatoms interact with light depends on their internal quantum states. This discovery gives researchers greater control over how quantum information moves through a system. They describe two different ways of connecting these structures to achieve useful outcomes.

In one setup, several giant superatoms are closely linked in a specific arrangement. This allows them to pass quantum states between each other without decoherence, meaning no information is lost.

In another setup, the atoms are spaced farther apart but connected in a carefully tuned way so that waves remain synchronized. This makes it possible to direct quantum signals and distribute entanglement over long distances.

Superatoms and giant atoms are engineered systems that behave like atoms rather than naturally occurring ones.

A superatom is a quantum system made up of multiple natural atoms that share a single quantum state and respond to light as one entity.

A giant atom, on the other hand, connects to light or sound waves at several separate points in space. It is called "giant" because it is larger than the wavelength of the light it interacts with.

Giant atoms have defined energy levels and follow the rules of quantum mechanics, yet they can reach sizes of up to millimeters, making them visible to the naked eye. Through electromagnetic or acoustic waves, they can interact with their surroundings at multiple locations at the same time. One way to picture this is as a single atom linked to a wave at several distant points. This unusual setup allows the atom to be influenced by the waves it produces.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10867. Taco Bell is bringing back the Steakhouse Boss Burrito after fans win its return

Taco Bell is bringing back a discontinued menu item after a decade, with the fast-food chain confirming that one of its former burritos will return to restaurants nationwide in 2027.

The announcement comes as Taco Bell continues to revive older menu items, including the Quesarito, Flamin’ Hot Grilled Cheese Burrito, and Cheesy Street Chalupas, which have recently returned for limited-time runs.

However, the latest comeback wasn’t decided by Taco Bell executives. Instead, the company allowed fans to choose which discontinued item would return as part of its inaugural Live Más Open golf tournament, hosted by comedian Kevin Hart.

Taco Bell has confirmed that the Steakhouse Boss Burrito will return to participating US restaurants for a limited time in 2027, marking its first appearance since 2016.

Originally introduced as the Steakhouse Burrito, the returning item features a warm flour tortilla filled with a choice of grilled, marinated steak or marinated all-white-meat chicken.

The burrito includes twice the amount of meat found in Taco Bell’s Steak Grilled Cheese Burrito, along with warm queso sauce, bacon, seasoned potatoes, and reduced-fat sour cream.

It also features a three-cheese blend consisting of mozzarella, pepper jack, and cheddar.

The decision to bring back the burrito came during Taco Bell’s Live Más Open, which took place at The Ranch at Laguna Beach in California earlier this month.

The nine-hole golf tournament featured celebrities, athletes, and Taco Bell fans competing for the opportunity to revive a discontinued menu item.

Team Pink, consisting of Taco Bell fan Abby Abel, professional skateboarder Sean Malto, influencer Emira D’Spain, and reality television personality Serena Page, secured the victory following a sudden-death putting playoff against Team White.

Taco Bell is making fans win a golf tournament to bring back a discontinued menu item

Taco Bell quietly tests Fire Piña Hot Dog on exclusive secret menu

Taco Bell revives Shredded Beef Dipping Taco alongside new beef loaded Nacho Fries

Their prize included championship jackets, a trophy, and the opportunity to select a former Taco Bell menu item for a nationwide comeback.

The tournament also featured a Taco Bell drive-thru on the golf course, serving exclusive items including the Fire Piña Hot Dog, Baja Caesar Crispy Chicken Crunchwrap, and Fiesta Potato Salad.

The Live Más Open is available to stream on Peacock beginning November 20, with a television broadcast scheduled for November 23 on Golf Channel.

Taco Bell has not announced an exact release date or pricing for the Steakhouse Boss Burrito, although the company confirmed it will be available nationwide for a limited time in 2027.

The announcement follows several other menu changes from Taco Bell, including the return of the Quesarito and the upcoming Make It Flamin’ Hot customization option, which will allow customers to add Flamin’ Hot Fritos to eligible menu items beginning November 24.

#10864. How Developers Can Improve Their Learning and Productivity With AI

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Artificial intelligence (AI) is changing how developers learn, write code, and manage their daily work. Tasks that once required hours of searching, testing, and manual effort can now be completed with help from AI. Developers can use it to understand complex programming concepts, find coding errors, learn new technologies, and complete repetitive tasks.

However, AI is not only useful for writing code faster. It can also help developers improve their technical knowledge, solve problems, and build better learning habits. When used correctly, it can become a useful assistant throughout the software development process.

The key is to understand where AI can help and where human skills are still needed. Here are several practical ways developers can use AI to improve their learning and productivity.

Learning a new programming language or understanding a difficult concept can take time. Developers often need to read long explanations, watch tutorials, and test different examples before they understand how something works.

AI can make this process easier by explaining technical concepts in simple language. Instead of searching through several websites, developers can ask direct questions and receive explanations based on their current knowledge.

For example, a developer learning JavaScript can ask AI to explain promises, callbacks, or asynchronous functions. It can break these topics into smaller parts and provide simple code examples to make them easier to understand.

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain the same concept in different ways. If one explanation is difficult to understand, a different example or comparison may make the idea clearer.

AI explanations are not always accurate, especially when dealing with complex programming concepts or unfamiliar technologies. Developers should verify important information before using it in their projects. Hallucination detection can help identify fabricated facts, incorrect technical explanations, and misleading information in AI-generated responses, making it easier to check the accuracy of what they learn.

This helps developers spend less time struggling with unfamiliar topics and more time building their technical knowledge.

Software development changes regularly. New frameworks, libraries, programming languages, and development methods continue to appear. Developers need to keep learning if they want to stay up to date.

AI can support this process by helping developers create learning plans based on their goals and current skills.

For example, a developer who wants to learn Python can ask AI to create a beginner-friendly learning plan. The plan can include basic syntax, variables, loops, functions, object-oriented programming, and small projects.

AI can also recommend practical exercises for each topic. Instead of only reading about a programming concept, developers can write code and test their understanding.

Here are some ways to use AI when learning new technologies:

However, developers should not depend entirely on AI-generated explanations. They should also read official documentation and test examples themselves. This helps them understand how technologies work in real development environments.

AI can help developers improve their coding skills by providing examples, explaining different solutions, and identifying areas that need improvement.

For example, a developer may write a function that works but contains unnecessary steps. They can ask AI to review the code and suggest ways to make it easier to read, maintain, or understand.

AI can also explain why one solution may be better than another. This allows developers to learn different approaches instead of always following the first solution they find.

Some practical ways to use AI for coding improvement include:

Developers can also ask AI to create small coding challenges based on their skill level. For example, someone learning Python can request exercises about lists, dictionaries, loops, and functions.

The important thing is to understand the code instead of copying it without review. Developers who examine the logic behind each solution can improve their programming knowledge over time.

Developers learn new information from many sources, including online courses, technical blogs, documentation, tutorials, and coding projects. Without a proper system, it can become difficult to find useful information when they need it again.

AI can help developers organize this knowledge and make learning resources easier to review.

For example, developers can use AI to summarize long technical explanations, create revision questions, and organize important programming concepts into smaller topics. They can also turn their learning notes into structured study guides that make it easier to revisit important information.

A well-organized knowledge system can be useful when learning a new framework or preparing for technical interviews. Instead of searching through old browser tabs or reading the same documentation repeatedly, developers can return to their saved explanations and notes.

Here are some useful ways to organize technical knowledge:

Technical documentation can contain detailed explanations that are difficult to remember. Developers can use AI to summarize important sections and turn them into short notes.

For example, when learning a new API, they can save information about its main functions, parameters, common errors, and practical examples.

Reading information does not always mean remembering it. Developers can ask AI to create questions from their notes so they can test their understanding.

For example, after learning about database indexing, they can create questions about how indexes work, when to use them, and how they affect query performance.

Developers should also save important information about their projects. This may include coding decisions, API details, database structures, and solutions to common problems.

Keeping these details organized makes it easier to return to a project after a break or help another developer understand the work.

Developers spend a large part of their working time on tasks that do not always require complex problem-solving. These may include writing basic code structures, creating test cases, preparing documentation, and handling repeated coding patterns.

AI can help reduce this manual effort by generating suggestions and automating parts of these tasks.

For example, when starting a new project, developers often need to create similar code structures, such as basic functions, configuration files, and reusable components. An AI coding assistant can generate an initial version that developers can review and adjust.

Automating these activities gives developers more time to focus on important parts of their projects, such as system design, complex features, and problem-solving.

However, generated code should always be checked before it is added to a project. Developers need to make sure it follows project requirements, coding standards, and security rules.

Finding and fixing bugs is an important part of software development. Even experienced developers can spend hours trying to understand why a program is not working as expected.

AI can help make debugging easier by explaining error messages, identifying possible causes, and suggesting solutions.

For example, when a developer encounters a Python error, they can share the relevant code and error message with an AI assistant. The assistant can explain what the error means and suggest possible ways to fix it.

AI can also help developers review code before submitting it. It may identify common issues such as:

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain why a particular bug occurs instead of only requesting a corrected version of the code.

This helps developers understand the problem and avoid making the same mistake in future projects.

Still, AI suggestions are not always correct. Developers should test proposed fixes, review the surrounding code, and confirm that the solution does not introduce new problems.

Managing several development tasks at once can be difficult. Developers may need to work on new features, fix bugs, review code, attend meetings, and meet project deadlines.

AI can help organize these responsibilities by breaking large tasks into smaller and more manageable steps.

For example, if a developer needs to build a user authentication system, they can ask AI to divide the work into separate tasks. These may include creating registration forms, setting up database tables, implementing login logic, adding password protection, and testing the system.

This makes it easier to understand the work involved and decide what needs attention first.

AI can also help explain technical requirements before development begins. This may reduce confusion and help developers identify missing details early.

However, AI-generated plans should be treated as starting points. Developers still need to consider their actual deadlines, technical limitations, and project requirements before following them.

Although AI offers many benefits, depending on it for every programming task can affect a developer's ability to solve problems independently.

If developers always ask AI to write code, fix errors, and explain every concept, they may miss opportunities to develop their own technical skills.

A 2026 Anthropic study found that participants using AI scored 17 percentage points lower on a programming knowledge quiz than participants who worked without AI in the experiment. This highlights why understanding the work still matters when using coding assistants.

Developers can avoid excessive dependence on AI by following a few simple practices:

When facing a coding problem, spend some time understanding it and trying possible solutions. If you get stuck, use AI to explore the issue rather than asking it to complete everything.

Never add AI-generated code to a project without understanding what it does. Review the logic, check the functions, and test the output.

AI can sometimes provide outdated information, incorrect code, or explanations that do not fit a particular project. Check important details against official documentation and reliable technical resources.

Regular coding practice without assistance helps developers maintain their problem-solving skills. It also shows whether they truly understand a programming concept.

The best approach is to use AI as a helpful assistant while keeping independent thinking and technical judgment at the center of development work.

Using AI occasionally can be helpful, but developers can get more value from it by making it part of a regular learning and working routine.

A simple daily routine can help developers balance their project responsibilities with continuous learning.

For example, developers can follow this approach:

Start with a learning goal: Spend some time understanding a new programming concept, framework, or technology. Use AI to explain difficult topics and provide practical examples.

Plan the day's development tasks: Ask AI to help organize important tasks and break complex work into smaller steps.

Use AI during development: Get help with unfamiliar code, debugging, test cases, and repetitive tasks. Review all suggestions before using them.

Save useful information: Keep important explanations, coding examples, and solutions in an organized knowledge base for future reference.

Review what you learned: At the end of the day, revisit important concepts and test your understanding through small questions or coding exercises.

Developers do not need to spend hours following this routine. Even a short period of focused learning each day can help them build knowledge while managing their regular development work.

AI is becoming a useful part of modern software development. It can help developers understand difficult concepts, learn new technologies, improve code quality, debug problems, and complete repetitive tasks more efficiently.

However, better productivity does not simply mean writing more code in less time. Developers also need to understand what they are building, improve their problem-solving skills, and keep learning as technology changes.

The most effective approach is to use AI for support rather than letting it make every decision. By combining AI assistance with regular practice, independent thinking, and organized learning, developers can improve both their technical skills and everyday productivity.

#10861. This simple change stops robot swarms from getting stuck

Imagine a swarm of robots rushing to complete an urgent job, such as cleaning up an oil spill or assembling complex machinery. At first, adding more robots speeds things up. But after a certain point, the space becomes crowded, robots start interfering with one another, and overall progress slows.

This raises a simple but important question: in a limited area, how many robots can you deploy before efficiency starts to drop? Researchers at Harvard believe they have found a clear answer.

A new study from the lab of L. Mahadevan, the Lola England de Valpine Professor of Applied Mathematics, Organismic and Evolutionary Biology, and Physics, shows that adding a controlled amount of randomness to how robots move can reduce congestion and improve performance in crowded environments.

The work combines mathematical modeling, computer simulations, and real-world experiments. It demonstrates how basic local movement rules can lead to organized, efficient outcomes on a larger scale. The findings could influence how robotic fleets are designed and may even apply to human crowd management and traffic flow. The research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences and led by applied mathematics Ph.D. student Lucy Liu, with guidance from SEAS Senior Research Fellow Justin Werfel.

Why Randomness Helps Predict Complex Behavior

Studying dense crowds is difficult because individuals can take countless possible paths and interact in unpredictable ways, Liu explained. To simplify the problem, the researchers treated each robot as a basic unit with a small, adjustable amount of variation in its movement.

"This might be counterintuitive, because how could randomness make things easier to work with?" said Liu. "But in this case, when you have a lot of randomness, it becomes possible to take averages -- average distances, average times, average behaviors. This makes it a lot easier to make predictions."

To explore this idea, the team created computer simulations of robot groups, referred to as agents. Each agent started at a random location and was assigned a random destination. Once it reached its target, it immediately received a new one, mimicking continuous task assignment in real-world systems.

Each agent moved toward its goal with a tunable amount of variation, described as "noise." With no noise, agents moved in straight lines. With high noise, their paths became erratic and inefficient. However, this wandering also helped them navigate around one another.

The simulations revealed a clear pattern. When agents moved in perfectly straight paths, they quickly formed dense clusters and traffic jams that halted progress. When movement became too random, congestion disappeared but efficiency dropped due to excessive wandering.

Between these extremes, the researchers identified a sweet spot. In this range, agents occasionally bumped into one another and formed short-lived clusters, but still managed to slip past and keep moving. This balance allowed the system to maintain a steady flow.

Using these insights, the team developed formulas to estimate "goal attainment rate," or how many destinations are reached over time. These equations made it possible to determine the ideal combination of crowd density and movement randomness to maximize performance.

To confirm their findings, Liu collaborated with physicist Federico Toschi at Eindhoven University of Technology in the Netherlands. Together, they set up experiments with small wheeled robots in a lab equipped with an overhead camera.

Each robot carried a QR code so its position could be tracked and updated with new destinations. Although the physical robots moved more slowly and less precisely than the simulated agents, they displayed the same overall patterns.

The experiments supported a key idea: highly complex coordination does not require advanced intelligence or centralized control. Instead, simple local rules can produce effective group behavior, at least within certain density limits.

"Understanding how active matter, whether it is a swarm of ants, a herd of animals, or a group of robots, become functional and execute tasks in crowded environments using the principles of self-organization, is relevant to many questions in behavioral ecology," Mahadevan said. "Our study suggests strategies that might well be much broader than the instantiation we have focused on."

Liu noted that she has long been interested in designing safer and more efficient crowded spaces. This research points toward a future where the movement of large groups, whether robots, vehicles, or people, could be predicted and optimized using mathematical tools.

The results suggest that introducing controlled variability into movement patterns may improve flow in many real-world systems, from factory floors to city streets.

Funding for the research came from the National Science Foundation Graduate Research Fellowship Program under Grant No. DGE 2140743, along with grants from the Simons Foundation and the Henri Seydoux Fund.

Materials provided by Harvard John A. Paulson School of Engineering and Applied Sciences. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
